3.5.
Separate typechecking

Our
Eliom
ε
calculus inherit all the properties of the
ML
calculus regarding
separate compilation. In particular, we show here that it trivially supports
separate typechecking, where each module is typechecked independently and only
requires knowledge of the module
types
(but not the implementations!) of its
dependencies.

Theorem 1 (Separate Typechecking).

Given a list of module declarations that form a typed program, there exists
an order such that each module can be typechecked with only knowledge
of the type of the previous modules.

More formally,
given a location
\varsigma

and a list of
n
declarations
D_{i}
and a signature
\mathcal{S}
such that

\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{\varsigma}(D_{1};\dots;D_{n})\operatorname{:}\mathcal{S}

then there exists
n
definitions
\mathcal{D}_{i}
and a permutation
\pi
such that

\displaystyle\forall\varsigma\forall i<n,\

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1};\dots;\mathcal{D}_{i}\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{\varsigma}D_{i+1}\operatorname{:}\mathcal{D}_{i+1}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{\varsigma}\mathcal{D}_{\pi(1)};\dots;\mathcal{D}_{\pi(n)}<:\mathcal{S}

Proof.

Proceed exactly as the
ML
proof for
Theorem

1
in
Section

A.2.3
.
∎

3.6.
Interpreted semantics

While
Eliom
, just like
OCaml
, is a compiled language, it is desirable
to present a semantics that does not involve complex program transformation.
The reason is two-fold: First, this simple semantics should be reasonably
easy to explain to users. Indeed, this semantics is the one used
to present
Eliom
in
Section

2
. However, we must also show
that this semantics is correct, in that it does actually
corresponds to our compilation scheme. This is done in
Section

5.5
.
As presented in
Section

2
,
Eliom
execution proceeds
in two steps: The server part of the program is executed first. This
creates a client program, which is then executed.

Let us first introduce a few notations.
Generated client programs are noted
\mu
. Server
expressions (resp. declarations) that do not contain injections
are noted
\overline{e}
(resp.
\overline{D}
).
Values are the same as for
ML
: constants, closures, structures
and functor closures.
We consider a new class of identifiers called “references” and noted in bold, such
as
\mathbf{r}
or
\mathbf{R}
.
We assume the existence of a name generator
that can create arbitrary new fresh
\mathbf{r}
identifiers at any point of the
execution. References are used as global identifiers that ignore
scoping rules.
References can also be qualified as “reference paths”, noted
\mathbf{X.r}
.
This is used for mixed functors, in particular.
We use
\gamma
to note the global environment where such
references are stored.

We now introduce a new reduction relation,

\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{\varsigma}
, which is the reduction over
Eliom
constructs
on side
\varsigma
.
The notation
\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{\varsigma}
actually represents several reduction relations
which are presented in
Figures

32
,
35
and
36
.
Four of these relations reduce the server part of the code and emit
a client program.
We note

e\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{\iota}v,\mu,\theta

the reduction of a server expression
e
inside a context
\iota
in the
environment
\rho
.
It returns the value
v
, the client program
\mu
and
emits the trace
\theta
.
The context
\iota
can be either base
(
b
), server (
s
), server code inside client contexts (
c/s
) or
server code inside mixed contexts (
m
).
We also have a client reduction, noted

e\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{c}v,\theta

which reduces a client expression
e
inside an environment
\rho
, returns
a value
v
and emits a trace
\theta
. It also updates a global environment
from
\gamma
to
\gamma^{\prime}
.

Note that the first family of relation executes
only
the
server part of a program and returns a client program, which is then executed
by
\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{c}
.
This is represented formally by the
Program
rule. In order to
reduce an
Eliom
program
P
, we first reduce the server part using

\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{m}
. This returns no value and a client program
\mu

which we execute.
We now look into each specific feature in greater detail

3.6.1.
Generated client programs

Let us first describe evaluation rules for generated client programs.
Generated client programs are
ML
programs with some additional constructions
which are described in
Figure

32
.
The new evaluation rules are presented in
Figure

32
.
The construction
\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{f}
binds the current accessible environment
to
\mathbf{f}
in the global environment
\gamma
. This is implemented
by the
BindEnv
rule.

\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{r}=e\ \texttt{with}\ \mathbf{f}
computes
e
in the environment previously associated to

\mathbf{f}
. The result is then stored as
\mathbf{r}
in
\gamma
.
This construction is also usable for module expressions
and is implemented by the
Bind
and
Bind
m
rules.
All these constructions also accept paths of references such as
\mathbf{R.f}
.

The new
\mathtt{bind}
constructs are similar to the ones
used in languages with continuations in the catch/throw style.
Instead of storing both an environment and the future computation, we
store only the environment. This will allow us to implement closures
across locations, in particular the case where fragments
are used inside a server closure.

The client reduction relation also inherits the
ML
rules
(rule
ClientCode
). In such a case, the global environment
is passed around following an order compatible with traces.
For example, the
LetIn
rule for let expression would
be modified like so:

\frac{e^{\prime}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}v^{\prime},\theta\quad e\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho+\left\{x\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}v^{\prime}\right\}\operatorname{|}\gamma^{\prime}\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime\prime}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}v,\theta^{\prime}}{(\mathtt{let}\ x=e^{\prime}\ \mathtt{in}\ e)\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime\prime}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}v,\theta\operatorname{@}\theta^{\prime}}

Here,
e^{\prime}
is evaluated first (since
\theta
is present first
in the resulting traces), hence it uses the initial environment
\gamma
and
returns the environment
\gamma^{\prime}
, which is then passed along.

From now on, we use
\mathbf{f}
to denote
the reference associated to fragments closures and
\mathbf{r}
to denote
the reference associated to a specific value of a fragment.

(Reference path)

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}::=\

\displaystyle(\mathbf{X.})^{*}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle D_{c}::=\

\displaystyle D_{{\textsc{ML}}}

(Env binding)

\displaystyle|\

\displaystyle\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{p}

(Global binding)

\displaystyle|\

\displaystyle\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{p}=e\ \texttt{with}\ \mathbf{p^{\prime}}\ |\ \mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{p}=M\ \texttt{with}\ \mathbf{p^{\prime}}

Figure 31
.
Grammar of client programs

\frac{e\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \gamma(\mathbf{p_{f}})\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}v,\theta\quad S\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}(\gamma^{\prime}+\left\{\mathbf{p}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}v\right\})\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime\prime}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}V,\theta^{\prime}}{(\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{p}=e\ \texttt{with}\ \mathbf{p_{f}};S)\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime\prime}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}V,\theta\operatorname{@}\theta^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{Bind}\frac{S\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}(\gamma+\left\{\mathbf{p_{f}}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\rho\right\})\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}V,\theta}{(\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{p_{f}};S)\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}V,\theta}\quad\textsc{BindEnv}\frac{M\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \gamma(\mathbf{p_{F}})\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}V,\theta\quad S\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}(\gamma^{\prime}+\left\{\mathbf{p}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}V\right\})\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime\prime}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}{(\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{p}=M\ \texttt{with}\ \mathbf{p_{F}};S)\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime\prime}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}V^{\prime},\theta\operatorname{@}\theta^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{Bind${}_{m}$}\frac{}{\text{Inherit the rules }\\
\\
\text{from {{ML}} }}\quad\textsc{ClientCode}

Figure 32
.
Semantics for client generated programs –

e\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}v,\theta

3.6.2.
Base, Client and Server declarations

We now consider the case of base, client and server declarations.
The rules are presented in
Figure

35
.
Let us first describe the execution of complete
Eliom
ε
programs
(rule
Program
). A program
P
reduces to a client value

v
if and only if we can first create a
server

reduction of
P
that produces no value, emits a client program
\mu

and a trace
\theta_{s}
. We can then create a reduction of
\mu

that reduces in
v
with a trace
\theta_{c}
. The trace of the program
is the concatenation of the traces.
We see that the execution of
Eliom
ε
program is split in two as described
earlier. Let us now look in more details at various construction
of the
Eliom
ε
language.

Base

The base reduction relation corresponds exactly to the
ML
reduction relation, and always returns empty programs (rule
BaseCode
).
When reducing a base declaration in a mixed context, we both reduce the
declaration using
\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{b}
, but also add the declaration to the emitted
client program (rule
BaseDecl
).
As we can see, base declarations are executed twice: once on the server
and once on the client.

Client contexts and injections

The goal of the client reduction relation
\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{c/s}

is
not
to reduce client programs. It only reduces server
code contained by injections inside client code.
It returns a client expression without injections, a client program
and a trace.
Since we don’t want to execute client code,
it does not inherit the reduction rules for
ML
.
Given an injection
f\mathtt{\%}e
, the rule
Injection

reduces the server side expression
(f^{s}\ e)
to a value
v
.
We then transform the server value
v
into a client value using the

\operatorname{\downarrow\!}{}
operator presented
Figure

34
.
We then returns the client expression
(f^{c}\ \operatorname{\downarrow\!}{v})
without executing it.
This expression will be executed on the client side, to deserialize the value.
The value injection operator, noted
\operatorname{\downarrow\!}{}
represents the serialization of values
from the server to the client
and is the identity over constants in
\text{Const}_{b}
and references, and fail on any other
values.
According to the definition of converters,
if
f
is a converter
\tau_{s}\operatorname{\leadsto}\tau_{c}
,
then
f^{s}
is the server side function of type
\mathrm{\tau_{s}}\operatorname{\rightarrow}\mathrm{\mathtt{serial}}

and
v
should be of type
\mathtt{serial}
. Since
\mathtt{serial}
is defined on
b
, the injection
of values should be the identity.

The rule
ClientContext
defines the evaluation of server expression up
to client contexts. Client contexts are noted
E[e_{1},\dots,e_{n}]
and are defined in

Figure

34
. A client context can have any number of holes which must
all contain injections. The rest of the context can contain arbitrary
ML
syntax that are not injections. Evaluation under a multi-holed context
proceed from left to right. The resulting programs and traces are constructed by
concatenation of each program and trace.

In order to evaluate client declarations, the rule
ClientDecl
uses
\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{c/s}

to evaluate the server code present in the declaration
D_{c}
which returns
a declaration without injections
\overline{D_{c}}
and a client program
\mu
.
We then return the client program composed by the concatenation of the two.
We demonstrate this in
5
.
The
Eliom
ε
program is presented on the left side.
It first declares the integer
a
on the server then inject it
on the client and returns the result.
The emitted code, shown in the middle, contains
an explicit call to the
\mathtt{int}^{c}
deserializer while the rest of the client
code is unchanged.
The returned value is shown on the right.

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{let}_{s}\ a=3\\
&\mathtt{let}_{c}\ \mathtt{return}=\mathtt{int}\mathtt{\%}x+1\end{aligned}\quad\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \hskip 7.97224pt\ }}_{m}\quad\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=(\mathtt{int}^{c}\ 3)+1\end{aligned}\quad\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \hskip 7.97224pt\ }}_{c}\quad 4,\langle\rangle

Server code and fragments

The server reduction relation reduces server code and emits
the appropriate client program associated to client fragments.
Since client program are mostly
ML
programs, it inherits the

ML
reduction rules (rule
ServerCode
)
where client programs are concatenated in the same order as traces.
Client fragments are handled by the rule
Fragment
.
Let us consider a fragment
\{\{\penalty\ e\penalty\ \}\}
,
this evaluation proceeds in two steps: first, we evaluate all the injections
inside the client expression
e
using the relation
\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{c/s}

described in the previous section. We thus
obtain an expression without injection
\overline{e}
and a client program

\mu
.

The second step is to register
\overline{e}
to be evaluated in the client program.
One could propose to simply consider client fragments as values. This is however
quite problematic, as it could lead to duplicated side effects. Consider
the program presented on the left side of
6
.
If we were simply to
simply pass fragments along, the
\mathtt{print}
statement would be evaluated twice.
Instead, we create a fresh identifier
\mathbf{r}
that will be
globally bound to
\overline{e}

in the client program, as shown in rule
Fragment
. This way, the client expression
contained inside the fragment will be executed once, in a timely manner.
The execution rule for fragment is demonstrated in
6
.
As before, the
Eliom
ε
program is presented on the left, the emitted
client program in shown in the middle and the returned value is on the right.
Note that both
\mathtt{frag}^{s}
and
\mathtt{frag}^{c}
are the identity function.

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{let}_{s}\ x=\{\{\penalty\ (\mathtt{print}\ 3)\penalty\ \}\}\\
&\mathtt{let}_{c}\ \mathtt{return}=\\
&\quad\mathtt{frag}\mathtt{\%}x+\mathtt{frag}\mathtt{\%}x\end{aligned}\quad\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \hskip 7.97224pt\ }}_{m}\quad\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{f}}\\
&\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{r}=(\mathtt{print}\ 3)\ \texttt{with}\ \mathbf{f}\\
&\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=\\
&\quad(\mathtt{frag}^{c}\ \mathbf{r})+(\mathtt{frag}^{c}\ \mathbf{r})\end{aligned}\quad\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \hskip 7.97224pt\ }}_{c}\quad 6,\langle 3\rangle

List of Examples 6

Execution of a fragment containing side-effects

Closures and fragments

In the client program above, we also use a reference
\mathbf{f}
and the

\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{}
construct. To see why this is necessary, we now consider
a case where fragments are used inside closures. This is presented
in
7
. The
Eliom
ε
program, presented
on the left, computes
1+3+2
on the client (although in a fairly
obfuscated way). We first define the client variable
a
as
1
.
We then define a server closure
f
containing a client fragment capturing
a
.
We then define a new variable also named
a
and call
(f\ 3)
, inject the results and returns.
When evaluating the definitions of
f
, since it contains syntactically
a client fragment, we will emit the client instruction
\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{f}
,
where
\mathbf{f}
is a fresh identifier. This will capture the local
environment, which is
\left\{a\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}1\right\}
at this point of the client program.
When we execute
(f\ 3)
, we will finally reduce the client
fragment and emit the
(\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{r}=(\mathtt{int}^{c}\ 3)+a\ \texttt{with}\ \mathbf{f})
instruction.
On the client, this will be executed in the
\mathbf{f}
environment,
hence
a
is
1
and the result is
4
.
Once this is executed, we move back to the regular environment, where
a

is
2
, and proceed with the execution.

Thanks to this construction, the capturing behavior of
closures is preserved across
location boundaries. The
\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{}
construct is
generated by the
ServerDecl
rule.
\operatorname{FRAGS}(D_{s})
returns
the fragments syntactically present in
D_{s}
.
For each fragment, the local environment is bound to the associated reference.

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{let}_{c}\ a=1\\
&\mathtt{let}_{s}\ f\ x=\{\{\penalty\ \mathtt{int}\mathtt{\%}x+a\penalty\ \}\}\\
&\mathtt{let}_{c}\ a=2\\
&\mathtt{let}_{s}\ y=(f\ 3)\\
&\mathtt{let}_{c}\ \mathtt{return}=\mathtt{frag}\mathtt{\%}y+a\end{aligned}\quad\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \hskip 7.97224pt\ }}_{m}\quad\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{let}\ a=1\\
&\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{f}}\\
&\mathtt{let}\ a=2\\
&\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{r}=(\mathtt{int}^{c}\ 3)+a\ \texttt{with}\ \mathbf{f}\\
&\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=(\mathtt{frag}^{c}\ \mathbf{r})+a\end{aligned}\quad\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \hskip 7.97224pt\ }}_{c}\quad 6,\langle\rangle

List of Examples 7

Figure 35
.
Semantics for base, client and server sections –

e\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varsigma}}v{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\mu},\theta

Fragment annotations

In the previous examples, we presented the server reduction rules
where, for each syntactic fragment, a fresh reference
\mathbf{f}
is
generated and bound to the environment. In the rest of this thesis,
we will simply assume that all fragments syntactically present in the
program are annotated with a unique reference. Such annotation
is purely syntactic and can be done by walking the syntax tree of the program.
Annotated fragments are noted
\{\{\penalty\ \dots\penalty\ \}\}_{\mathbf{f}}
.

Mixed structures syntactically present in the program are also annotated
in a similar manner with a unique module reference. Annoted
mixed structures are noted
\mathtt{struct}\ \dots\ \mathtt{end}_{\mathbf{F}}
.

3.6.3.
Mixed modules

Let us now describe the reduction relation for mixed modules.
The mixed reduction relation is presented in
Figure

36
and,
just like the server relation, has for goal
to evaluate all the server code and emit a client program to be later
evaluated by the client relation.
Mixed modules can be composed of either mixed functors, functor applications
or structures. The mixed relation contains various rules that are similar
to the
ML
reduction rules for modules. The notable novel aspect
of mixed functor is that they both have a client part and a server part.
This is different from client fragments, which only have a client part that
can be manipulated on the server via an identifier.
The server part of mixed modules also need to indicate its client part. In order
to do this, each mixed structure will contains an additional field called

\mathtt{Dyn}
which contains a module identifier. The identifier points to a
globally bound module on the client which is the result of the client-side
evaluation.

Let us first demonstrate these features in
8
.
In this example, we declare a mixed module
X
containing
a fragment
x
and an integer
y
. We then declare another mixed module
Y

containing a submodule. The structure of the emitted client code
mimics closely the structure of the server code. In particular, the
\mathtt{bind}

operation is nested inside the mixed module
X
that is emitted on the client.
The exact same names are reused on the client. We also register
each structure in the global environment using the annotated identifier of
the structure. Here, we use the
\mathtt{bind}
construct
as a shorthand for
\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{with}
that doesn’t change the
environment.
The shape of the program is kept intact thanks to the

MixedModVar
,
MixedQualModVar
and
MixedStruct
rules.
The first two are similar to the non mixed version, but the last one
deserves some explanation.
First, it prefixes all the fragment references inside the body of the
structure. This is for consistency with functors, as we will see later.
It then adds the
\mathtt{Dyn}
field to the returned structure, as discussed before.
Finally, it emits a
\mathtt{bind}
on the client
and returns the module reference. Each structure is thus bound
appropriately, even when nested.

Module identifiers are not used in the present program, but they are
used in the case of mixed functors, as we will see now.

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{module}_{m}\ X=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\quad\mathtt{let}_{s}\ x=\{\{\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \}\}\\
&\quad\mathtt{let}_{c}\ y=2+\mathtt{frag}\mathtt{\%}x\\
&\mathtt{end}_{\mathbf{X}}\\
&\mathtt{module}_{m}\ Y=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\quad\mathtt{module}_{m}\ A=X\\
&\mathtt{end}_{\mathbf{Y}}\\
&\mathtt{let}_{c}\ \mathtt{return}=Y.A.y\end{aligned}\quad\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \hskip 7.97224pt\ }}_{m}\quad\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{X}=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\quad\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{X.f}\\
&\quad\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{r}=1\ \texttt{with}\ \mathbf{X.f}\\
&\quad\mathtt{let}\ y=2+(\mathtt{frag}^{c}\ \mathbf{r})\\
&\mathtt{end}\\
&\mathtt{module}\ X=\mathbf{X}\\
&\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{Y}=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\quad\mathtt{module}\ A=X\\
&\mathtt{end}\\
&\mathtt{module}\ Y=\mathbf{Y}\\
&\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=Y.A.y\end{aligned}\quad\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \hskip 7.97224pt\ }}_{c}\quad 3,\langle\rangle

List of Examples 9

Execution of mixed functors with injections

Before exposing the complex interaction of mixed functors and fragment, let us
illustrate various details about mixed functors in
9
.
The server code proceed in the following way: we first define
a server variable
x
followed by a mixed functor
F
containing an
injection. We then define a mixed module
Y
and executes
on the client

the functor application
F(Y)
.

First, let us recall that injections inside mixed functors
can only refer to elements outside the functor. This means
that injections inside functors can be reduced as soon
as we consider a functor. In particular, we do not wait
for functor application. This can be seen in
the
ModClosure
rule which returns
a functor closure on the server side and emit the client
part of the functor on the client side.
We then take the client part of
the body of the functor (noted
M\rvert_{c}
)
and applies the
\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{c/s}
reduction relation,
which executes injections inside client code.
In this example, it results in the injection
\mathtt{int}\mathtt{\%}x
being
resolved immediately in the client-side version of the functor.

Mixed functor application can be done in client and server contexts.
When it is done in a
client context, we simply call the client-side definition
and omits the server-side execution completely. Hence we can simply
emit the client-code
F(Y)
. Execution is done through the
usual rules for client sections. This is always valid since
each mixed declaration emits a client declaration with the same name
and the same shape.

Mixed functors and fragments

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{module}_{m}\ F(X:\mathcal{M})=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\quad\mathtt{let}_{s}\ x=\{\{\penalty\ X.a+\mathtt{int}\mathtt{\%}X.b\penalty\ \}\}_{\mathbf{f_{x}}}\\
&\mathtt{end}_{\mathbf{F}}\\
&\mathtt{module}_{m}\ Y=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\quad\mathtt{let}_{c}\ a=4\\
&\quad\mathtt{let}_{s}\ b=2\\
&\mathtt{end}_{\mathbf{Y}}\\
\\
&\mathtt{module}_{m}\ Z=F(Y)\\
\\
&\mathtt{let}_{c}\ \mathtt{return}=\mathtt{frag}\mathtt{\%}Z.x\end{aligned}\ \operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ \ }}_{m}\ \begin{aligned} &\\
&\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{F}\\
&\mathtt{module}\ F(X:\mathcal{M})=\mathtt{struct}\ \ \mathtt{end}\\
&\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{Y}=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\quad\mathtt{let}\ a=4\\
&\mathtt{end}\\
&\mathtt{module}\ Y=\mathbf{Y}\\
\\
&\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{R_{Z}}=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\quad\mathtt{module}\ X=\mathbf{Y}\\
&\quad\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{R_{Z}.f_{x}}\\
&\quad\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{r_{x}}=Y.a+(\mathtt{int}^{c}\ 2)\\
&\quad\ \ \mathtt{with}\ \mathbf{R_{Z}.f_{x}}\\
&\mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{with}\ \mathbf{F}\\
&\mathtt{module}\ Z=F(Y)\\

&\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=(\mathtt{frag}^{c}\ \mathbf{r_{x}})\end{aligned}\ \operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ \ }}_{c}\ 6,\langle\rangle

List of Examples 10

Execution of mixed functors with fragments

The difficulty of the reduction of mixed functor containing
fragments is that the
server-side application of a mixed functor should result in both
server and client effects. This makes the reduction
rules for mixed functor application quite delicate.
We illustrate this with
10
.
In this example, we define a functor
F
contains only the server
declaration
x
. The argument of the functor simply
contains two integers, one on the server and one on the client.
In the fragment bound to
x
, we add the two integers (using
an escaped value). The interesting aspect here is that the body of
the client fragment depends on both the client and the server side
of the argument, even if there is no actual client side for the functor
F
.
The rest of the program is composed of a simple mixed module
Y
and the
mixed functor application
F(Y)
.

Mixed module expressions

\frac{S\!\left[\mathbf{f_{i}}\mapsto\mathbf{X.f_{i}}\right]_{i}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\mu},\theta\quad V^{\prime}=V+\left\{\mathtt{Dyn}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\mathbf{X}\right\}}{\mathtt{struct}\ S\ \mathtt{end}_{\mathbf{X}}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\mathbf{X},\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{X}=\mathtt{struct}\ \mu\ \mathtt{end}},\theta}\quad\textsc{MixedStruct}\frac{\rho(X)=V}{X\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},X,\varepsilon},\langle\rangle}\quad\textsc{MixedModVar}\frac{M\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},M_{c},\mu},\theta\quad M^{\prime}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},M^{\prime}_{c},\mu^{\prime}},\theta^{\prime}\quad V(V^{\prime})\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V^{\prime\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\mu^{\prime\prime}},\theta^{\prime\prime}}{M(M^{\prime})\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V^{\prime\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},M_{c}(M^{\prime}_{c}),\mu;\mu^{\prime};\mu^{\prime\prime}},\theta\operatorname{@}\theta^{\prime}\operatorname{@}\theta^{\prime\prime}}\quad\textsc{App}\frac{\mathbf{R}\text{ fresh}\quad V_{f}=\mathtt{functor}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}(\rho^{\prime})(X_{i}\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i}\mathtt{struct}\ S\ \mathtt{end}_{\mathbf{F}}\quad V_{i}(\mathtt{Dyn})=\mathbf{R_{i}}\quad S\!\left[\mathbf{f_{i}}\mapsto\mathbf{R.f_{i}}\right]_{i}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho^{\prime}+\left\{X_{i}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}V_{i}\right\}_{i}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\mu},\theta}{V_{f}(V_{1})\dots(V_{n})\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V+\left\{\mathtt{Dyn}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\mathbf{R}\right\}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\left(\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{R}=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\quad(\mathtt{module}\ X_{i}=\mathbf{R_{i}};)_{i}\\
&\quad\mu\\
&\mathtt{end}\ \mathtt{with}\ \mathbf{F}\end{aligned}\right)},\theta}\quad\textsc{StructBeta}\frac{V=\mathtt{functor}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}(\rho^{\prime})(X_{i}\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i}M\quad\quad M\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho^{\prime}+\left\{X_{i}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}V_{i}\right\}_{i}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V_{r}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\mu},\theta}{V(V_{1})\dots(V_{n})\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V_{r}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\mu,\theta},}\quad\textsc{NotStructBeta}\frac{p\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\mu},\theta}{p.X\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V(X){\color[rgb]{0,0,1},p.X,\mu},\theta}\quad\textsc{MixedQualModVar}\frac{}{\varepsilon\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}\{\}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\varepsilon},\langle\rangle}\quad\textsc{Empty}\frac{M\rvert_{c}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c/s}}\overline{M}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\mu},\theta}{\mathtt{functor}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}(X\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M})M\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}\mathtt{functor}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}(\rho)(X\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M})M{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\mathtt{functor}(X\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}\rvert_{c})\overline{M},\mu},\theta}\quad\textsc{ModClosure}

Mixed declarations

\frac{\operatorname{MixedStructIds}(M)=\overline{\mathbf{F_{i}}}\quad M\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},M^{c},\mu},\theta\quad S\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho+\left\{X\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}V\right\}\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}V^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\mu^{\prime}},\theta^{\prime}}{\mathtt{module}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}\ X=M;\ S\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m}}\left\{X\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}V\right\}+V^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},(\overline{\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{F_{i}}};\mu;\mathtt{module}\ X=M^{c};\mu^{\prime})},\theta\operatorname{@}\theta^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{MixedModDecl}

Figure 36
.
Semantics for mixed modules –

M\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varsigma}}V{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},\mu},\theta

The first step of the execution is to define the client side part of
F
and

Y
, as demonstrated in the previous example.
In this case, since
F
only contains a server side declaration, the client
part of the functor returns an empty structure.
We then have to
execute
F(Y)
. This is done with the
StructBeta
rule.
When reducing a mixed functor application, we first generate a fresh
identifier (
\mathbf{R_{Z}}
here) and prefix all the fragment closure identifiers.
We then evaluate the body of the functor on the server, which gives us
both the server module value and the generated client code.
In this case, we simply obtain the binding of
\mathbf{r_{x}}
. Note
that this reference is not prefixed by
\mathbf{R_{Z}}
since it
is freshly generated at runtime. If the functor was applied
again, we would simply generated a new one.
In order for functor arguments to be properly available on the client,
we need to introduce additional bindings. For this purpose, we lookup
the
\mathtt{Dyn}
field for each module argument and insert the
additional binding. In this case,
\mathtt{module}\ X=\mathbf{Y}
.
This gives us a complete client structure which we can bind to
\mathbf{R_{Z}}
.

We see here that the body of functors allows to emit client code
in a dynamic but controlled way. Generated module references used on the
client are remembered on the server using the
\mathtt{Dyn}
field while
closure identifiers ensure that the proper environment is used.
One problematic aspect of this method is that it leads to two
executions of the client side. A partial work-around is to keep track of
loaded client module to avoid duplicating side-effects.

4.
Integration with
OCaml

We now formalize more precisely the interaction between
Eliom
ε
, its location
system, and the vanilla
ML
language.
In particular,
Theorem

6
shows that
ML
modules
can be completely embedded in
Eliom
ε
programs without changes.

Let us note

\mathcal{M}_{\left[\ell\mapsto\ell^{\prime}\right]}
the
substitution on locations in an
Eliom
module type
\mathcal{M}
.
Given an
ML
module
M
, we note
M_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell\right]}
the
Eliom
module where all the module components have been annotated with location

\ell
.
Given en
Eliom
module
M
, we note
M_{\left[\mapsto{\textsc{ML}}\right]}
the
ML
module
where all the location have been erased.
We extend these notations to module types and environments.

4.1.
Properties of specialization

Let us first clarify the behavior of specialization with regard to mono-located
Eliom
ε
module types.

Proposition 0.

Given an
Eliom
module type
\mathcal{M}
and a location
\ell\in\{b,c,s\}
,
if
\Gamma\operatorname{\vDash}_{\ell}\mathcal{M}
, then
\lfloor\mathcal{M}\rfloor_{\ell}=\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

By definition of
\operatorname{<:}
,
\mathcal{M}
can only contain declarations on
\ell
.
This means that, by reflexivity of
\operatorname{\succ}
, only specialization rules
that leave the declaration unchanged are involved.
∎

Proposition 0.

Given an
Eliom
module type
\mathcal{M}
and a location
\ell\in\{c,s\}
,
if
\Gamma\operatorname{\vDash}_{b}\mathcal{M}
,
then
\lfloor\mathcal{M}\rfloor_{\ell}=\mathcal{M}_{\left[b\mapsto\ell\right]}
.

Proof.

We remark that for all
\ell\in\{c,s\}
,
b\operatorname{\succ}\ell
.
Additionally, mixed functors cannot appear on base
(since
m\operatorname{\nsucc}b
).
We can then proceed by induction over the rules for specialization.
∎

4.2.
Interaction between
ML
and
Eliom
ε

We can now relate
ML
code to equivalent
Eliom
ε
code that has been annotated
with a single location.

Proposition 0.

Given
ML
type
\tau
, expression
e
, module
M
and module type
\mathcal{M}
and
locations
\ell
,
\ell^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\Gamma\operatorname{\vDash}_{{\textsc{ML}}}\tau

\displaystyle\implies\Gamma_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell^{\prime}\right]}\operatorname{\vDash}_{\ell}\tau

\displaystyle\text{Where }\ell^{\prime}\operatorname{\succ}\ell

\displaystyle\Gamma\operatorname{\triangleright}_{{\textsc{ML}}}e\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\tau}

\displaystyle\implies\Gamma_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell^{\prime}\right]}\operatorname{\triangleright}_{\ell}e\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\tau}

\displaystyle\text{Where }\ell^{\prime}\operatorname{\succ}\ell

\displaystyle\Gamma\operatorname{\vDash}_{{\textsc{ML}}}\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\implies\Gamma_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell^{\prime}\right]}\operatorname{\vDash}_{\ell}\mathcal{M}_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell\right]}

\displaystyle\text{Where }\ell^{\prime}\operatorname{\succ}\ell

\displaystyle\Gamma\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{{\textsc{ML}}}M\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\implies\Gamma_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell^{\prime}\right]}\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{\ell}M_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell\right]}\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell\right]}

\displaystyle\text{Where }\ell^{\prime}\operatorname{\succ}\ell

Proof.

We remark that each syntax, typing rule or well formedness rule for

ML
has a direct equivalent rule in
Eliom
.
We can then simply rewrite the proof tree of the hypothesis to use
the
Eliom
type and well-formedness rules.
We consider only some specific cases:

•

By
Proposition

1
and since the modules are of uniform
location, the specialization operation in
Var
and
ModVar

are the identity.

•

The side conditions
\ell^{\prime}\operatorname{<:}\ell
are always respected
since the modules are of uniform location and by reflexivity of
\operatorname{<:}
.

•

The side conditions
\ell^{\prime}\operatorname{\succ}\ell
are respected by hypothesis.∎

Proposition 0.

Given
ML
type
\tau
, expression
e
, module
m
and module type
\mathcal{M}
:

\displaystyle\Gamma\operatorname{\vDash}_{b}\tau

\displaystyle\implies\Gamma_{\left[\mapsto{\textsc{ML}}\right]}\operatorname{\vDash}_{{\textsc{ML}}}\tau

\displaystyle\Gamma\operatorname{\vDash}_{b}\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\implies\Gamma_{\left[\mapsto{\textsc{ML}}\right]}\operatorname{\vDash}_{{\textsc{ML}}}\mathcal{M}_{\left[\mapsto{\textsc{ML}}\right]}

\displaystyle\Gamma\operatorname{\triangleright}_{b}e\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\tau}

\displaystyle\implies\Gamma_{\left[\mapsto{\textsc{ML}}\right]}\operatorname{\triangleright}_{{\textsc{ML}}}e\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\tau}

\displaystyle\Gamma\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{b}M\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\implies\Gamma_{\left[\mapsto{\textsc{ML}}\right]}\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{{\textsc{ML}}}M_{\left[\mapsto{\textsc{ML}}\right]}\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}_{\left[\mapsto{\textsc{ML}}\right]}

Proof.

We first remark that the following features are forbidden in the base part
of the language: injections, fragments, mixed functors and any other location
than base. The rest of the language contains no tierless features and
coincides with
ML
. We can then proceed by induction over the proof trees.
∎

Proposition 0.

Given an
ML
module
M
(resp. expression
e
),
an execution environment
\rho
, a location
\ell
, a value
V
(resp.
v
) and
a trace
\theta
:

\displaystyle M\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}V{},\theta

\displaystyle\iff M_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell\right]}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell\right]}\ }}_{\ell}V_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell\right]},\varepsilon,\theta

\displaystyle e\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}v{},\theta

\displaystyle\iff e_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell\right]}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell\right]}\ }}_{\ell}v_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\ell\right]},\varepsilon,\theta

Furthermore, given an
ML
program
P
,
an execution environment
\rho
, a value
v
and a trace
\theta
:

\displaystyle P\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}v{},\theta

\displaystyle\iff P_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\iota\right]}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\iota\right]}\ }}v_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto\iota\right]},\theta

\displaystyle\iota\in\{c,s\}

\displaystyle P\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}v{},\theta

\displaystyle\iff P_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto b\right]}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto b\right]}\ }}v_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto b\right]},\theta\operatorname{@}\theta

Proof.

Let us first note that the
ML
reduction relation is included in the base, the server
and the client-only relations.
Additionally, the considered programs, modules or expressions can not contain fragments,
injections or binds.
The additional rules in the server and client-only relations are only used
for these additional syntactic constructs.
For the first three statements, we can then proceed by induction.
For the last statement, we remark that base code is completely copied to the client
during server execution. Using rule
Program
, we execute the program
twice, which returns the same value but duplicates the trace.
∎

Theorem 6 (Base/
ML
correspondance).

Eliom
modules, expressions and types on base location
b
correspond
exactly to the
ML
language.

Proof.

By
Propositions

3
,
4
and
5
.
∎

Thanks to
Theorem

6
, we can completely identify the language

ML
and the part of
Eliom
on base location.
This is of course by design: the base location allows us to reason about
the host language,
OCaml
, inside the new language
Eliom
.
It also provides
the guarantee that anything written in the base location does not contain any
communication between client and server.
In the rest of the thesis, we omit location substitutions of the form

{}_{\left[{\textsc{ML}}\mapsto b\right]}
and
{}_{\left[b\mapsto{\textsc{ML}}\right]}
.

Proposition

3
also has practical consequences:
Given a file previously typechecked by an
ML
typechecker, we can directly use the module types either on base, but also
on the client or on the server, by simply annotating all the signature
components.
This give us the possibility, in the implementation, to completely
reuse compiled objects from the
OCaml
typechecker and load them
on an arbitrary location.
In particular, it guarantees that we can reuse pure
OCaml
libraries
safely and freely.

5.
Compilation of
Eliom
programs

In
Section

3
, we gave a tour of the
Eliom
ε
language
from a formal perspective, providing a type system and an interpreted semantics.

Eliom
, however, is not an interpreted language. The interpreted semantics
is here both as a formal tool and to make the semantics of the language more
approachable to users, as demonstrated in
Section

2
.
In the implementation,
Eliom
programs are compiled to two programs: one server program
(which is linked and executed on the server) and a client program (which is
compiled to
JavaScript
and executed in a browser). The resulting programs are efficient
and avoid unnecessary back-and-forth communications between the client and the server.

Description of the complete compilation toolchain, including emission of

JavaScript
code, is out of scope of this article (see
Vouillon and
Balat 2014
).
Instead, we describe
the compilation process in term of emission of client and server
programs in an
ML
-like language equipped with additional primitives.
Hence, we present
the typing and execution of compiled programs, in
Section

5.1

and the compilation process, in
Section

5.2
.
We then show that our compilation process preserves both the typing
and the semantics in
Section

5.5
.

5.1.
Target languages
ML
s
and
ML
c

We introduce the two target languages
ML
c
and
ML
s
as extensions of
ML
.
The additions in these two new languages are highlighted in
Figure

37
.
Typing is provided in
Section

5.1.5
.
The semantics is provided in
Section

5.1.6
.
As before, we use globally bound identifiers, which we call “references”
and note in bold:
\mathbf{r}
. References can also be paths, such as
\mathbf{X.r}
.
In some contexts, we accept a special form of reference path,
noted
\mathtt{Dyn}.\mathbf{x}
which we explain in
Section

5.1.4
.
In practice, these references are implemented
with uniquely generated names and associative tables.
Contrary to the interpreted semantics, references are also used to
transfer values from the server to the client and can appear
in expressions.
A reference used inside an expression is always of type
\mathtt{serial}
.

5.1.1.
Converters

For each converter
f
, we note
f^{s}
and
f^{c}
the server side encoding
function and the client side decoding function. If
f
is of type

\tau_{s}\operatorname{\leadsto}\tau_{c}
, then
f^{s}
is of type
\mathrm{\tau_{s}}\operatorname{\rightarrow}\mathrm{\mathtt{serial}}
and

f^{c}
is of type
\mathrm{\mathtt{serial}}\operatorname{\rightarrow}\mathrm{\tau_{c}}
.
We will generally assume that if the converter
f
is available in
the environment, then
f^{c}
and
f^{s}
are available in the client
and server environment respectively.

5.1.2.
Injections

For injections, we associate server-side the injected
value
e
to a reference
\mathbf{v}
using the construction
\mathtt{injection}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{v}}\ e
,
where
e
is of type
\mathtt{serial}
.
When the server execution is over, a mapping from references to injected values
is sent to the client.
\mathbf{v}
is then used client-side to access the value.

An example is given in
11
. In this example, two integers are
sent from the server to the client and add them on the client.
We suppose the existence of a base abstract type
\mathtt{int}
, a converter

\mathtt{int}
of type
(\mathtt{int}\operatorname{\leadsto}\mathtt{int})
and the associated encoding and decoding functions.
The server program, in
Figure

38(a)
, creates two injections,
\mathbf{v_{1}}

and
\mathbf{v_{2}}
and does not expose any bindings nor return any values. These injections
hold the serialized integers
4
and
2
.
The client program, in
Figure

38(b)
, uses these two injections, deserialize
their values, adds them, and returns the result.
Note that
\mathtt{injection}

is not a network operation
. It simply stores a mapping
between references (
i.e.
, names) and serialized values. The mapping generated at the
end of the server execution is shown in
Figure

38(c)
. After the
execution of the server code, this mapping is sent to the client, and used to execute
the client code.

\displaystyle\mathtt{injection}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{v_{1}}}\ (\mathtt{int}^{s}\ 4);

\displaystyle\mathtt{injection}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{v_{2}}}\ (\mathtt{int}^{s}\ 2);

(a)
Server program

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt(\mathtt{int}^{c}\ \mathbf{v_{1}})+(\mathtt{int}^{c}\ \mathbf{v_{2}});

(b)
Client program

\displaystyle\mathbf{v_{1}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\ 4

\displaystyle\mathbf{v_{2}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\ 2

(c)

Client-server programs calling and using injections

5.1.3.
Fragments

The primitive related to fragments also relies on shared references between
the server program and the client program. However, these references
allow to uniquely identify functions that are defined on the client but
are called on the server.
To implement this, we use the following primitives:

•

In
ML
c
structures,
\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{p}=e
declares a new client function bound
to the reference
\mathbf{p}
.
The function
e
takes an arbitrary amount of argument of type

\mathtt{serial}
and returns any type.

•

In
ML
s
expressions,
\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{p}\ e_{1}\dots e_{n}
is a delayed function application
which registers that, on the client, the function associated to
\mathbf{p}
will
be applied to the
arguments
e_{i}
. All the arguments must be of type
\mathtt{serial}
. It returns a
value of type
\mathtt{frag}
, which holds a unique identifier refering to the result of this application.

Here again, none of these
primitives are network communication primitives. While the API
is similar to Remote Procedure Calls, the execution is very different:

\mathtt{fragment}
only accumulates
the function call in a list, to be executed later. When the server execution
is over,
the list of calls is sent to the client, and used during the client
execution.

OCaml
, and consequently
Eliom
, are impure languages: the order of execution
of statement is important. In order to control the order of execution, we
introduce two additional statements:
\mathtt{end}\ (){}
, on the server, introduces
an
\mathtt{end}
marker in the list of calls.
\mathtt{exec}\ (){}
, on the client, executes
all the calls until the next
\mathtt{end}
token.

12
presents a pair of programs which
emit the client trace
\langle 2;3;3\rangle
, but in such a way that, while
the client does the printing, the values and the execution
order are completely determined by the server.
The server code
(
Figure

39(a)
) calls
\mathbf{f}
with
2
as argument, injects
the result and then calls
\mathbf{f}
with
3
as argument.
The client code, in
Figure

39(b)
, declares a fragment
closure
\mathbf{f}
, which simply adds one to its arguments, and exec both fragments.
In-between both executions, it prints the content of the injection
\mathbf{v}
.
During the execution of the server, the list of calls
(
Figure

39(d)
) and the mapping of injections
(
Figure

39(d)
) are built.
First, when
\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{f}\ (\mathtt{int}^{s}\ 2)
is executed, a fresh
reference
\mathbf{r_{1}}
is generated, the call to the fragment is added
to the list and
\mathbf{r_{1}}
is returned.
The injection adds the association
\mathbf{v_{1}}\mapsto\mathbf{r_{1}}
to the
mapping of injections.
The call to
\mathtt{end}\ ()
then adds the token
\mathtt{end}
to the list of fragments.
The second fragment proceeds similarly to the first, with a fresh
identifiers
\mathbf{r_{2}}
.
Once server execution is over, the newly generated list of fragments
and mapping of injections are sent to the client.
During the client execution, the
execution of the list is controlled by the
\mathtt{exec}
calls.
First,
(\mathbf{f}\ 2)
emits
\langle 2\rangle
and
is evaluated to
3
, and the mapping
\mathbf{r_{1}}\mapsto 3
is added to a global
environment. Then
\mathbf{v_{1}}
is resolved to
\mathbf{r_{1}}
and printed
(which shows
\langle 3\rangle
).
Finally
(\mathbf{f}\ 3)
emits
\langle 3\rangle
and is evaluated to
4
.

The important thing to note here is that both the injection mapping and the
list of fragments are completely dynamic. We could add complicated control
flow to the server program that would drive the client execution
according to some dynamic values.
The only static elements are
the names
\mathbf{f}
and
\mathbf{v_{1}}
,
the behavior of
\mathbf{f}

and the number of call to
\mathtt{exec}\ ()
.
We cannot, however, make the server-side control flow depend on
client-side values, since we obey a strict phase separation between
server and client execution.

Finally, remark that we do not need the
\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}\ \mathbf{}
construct
introduced in
Section

3.6.1
. Instead, we directly
capture the environment using closures that are extracted in advance.
We will see how this extraction works in more details while studying
the compilation scheme, in
Section

5.2
.

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}\ x_{1}=\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{f}\ (\mathtt{int}^{s}\ 2);

\displaystyle\mathtt{injection}\ \mathbf{v_{1}}\ (\mathtt{frag}^{s}\ x_{1});

\displaystyle\mathtt{end}\ (){};

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}\ x_{2}=\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{f}\ (\mathtt{int}^{s}\ 3);

\displaystyle\mathtt{end}\ (){};

(a)
Server program

\displaystyle\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{f}=\lambda x.((\mathtt{print}\ (\mathtt{int}^{c}\ x))+1);

\displaystyle\mathtt{exec}\ (){};

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}\ a=(\mathtt{print}\ (\mathtt{frag}^{c}\ \mathbf{v_{1}}));

\displaystyle\mathtt{exec}\ (){};

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=a

(b)
Client program

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{r_{1}}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}(\mathbf{f}\ 2)\right\};\mathtt{end};

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{r_{2}}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}(\mathbf{f}\ 3)\right\};\mathtt{end};

(c)
List of fragments

\displaystyle\mathbf{v_{1}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\ \mathbf{r_{1}}

(d)

5.1.4.
Modules

We introduce three new module-related construction that
are quite similar to fragment primitives:

•

\mathtt{bind}_{m}\ \mathbf{p}=M
is equivalent to
\mathtt{bind}
for modules. It is a client
instruction that associates the module or functor
M
to the reference
\mathbf{p}
.

•

\mathtt{fragment}_{m}\ \mathbf{p}\ (\mathbf{R_{1}})\dots(\mathbf{R_{n}})
is analogous to
\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{p}\ e
for modules.
It is a delayed functor application that is used on the server to register that the functor associated to
\mathbf{p}
will have to be applied to the modules
associated to
\mathbf{R_{i}}
. It returns
a fresh reference that represents the resulting module. Contrary to
\mathtt{fragment}
,
it can only be applied to module references.

•

p.\mathtt{Dyn}
returns a reference that represents the client part of a server
module
p
. This is used for
Eliom
ε
mixed structure that have both a server
and a client part.

The first argument of
\mathtt{fragment}
,
\mathtt{fragment}_{m}
,
\mathtt{bind}
and
\mathtt{bind}_{m}

can also be a reference path
\mathtt{Dyn}.\mathbf{f}
, where
\mathtt{Dyn}
is the locally bound

\mathtt{Dyn}
field inside a module. This allows us to isolate some bound
references inside a fresh module reference. This is useful
for functors, as we will now demonstrate in
13
.

In this example, we again add integers
2
2

2

But better! or at very least, more obfuscated.

on the client
while controlling the values and the control flow on the server.
We want to define server modules that contain server values but also
trigger some evaluation on the client, in a similar way to fragments.
The first step is to define a module
X
on the server and to bind
a corresponding module
\mathbf{X}
on the client. Similarly to the
interpreted semantics presented in
Section

3.6
, we
add a
\mathtt{Dyn}
field to the server module that points to the client
module. Plain structures such as
X
are fairly straightforward,
as we only need to declare each part statically and add the needed
reference.
\mathtt{bind}_{m}
allows to declare modules globally.

We then declare the functor
F
on the server and bind
the functor
\mathbf{F}
on the client. The server-side functor contains a call
to a fragment defined in the client-side functor. The difficulty here
is that we should take care of differentiating between fragment
closures produced by different functor applications.
For this purpose, we use a similar technique than the one presented
in
Section

3.6.3
, which is to prefix
the fragment closure identifier
\mathbf{f}
with the reference of the
client-side module. This reference is available on the server
side as the
\mathtt{Dyn}
field and is generated by a call to
\mathtt{fragment}_{m}
.
When
F
is applied to
X
on the server, we generate a fresh reference

\mathbf{R}
and add
\left\{\mathbf{R_{1}}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\mathbf{F}\ \mathbf{X_{0}}\right\}
to the execution queue.
When
\mathtt{exec}\ ()
is called, We introduce the additional binding

\left\{\mathtt{Dyn}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\mathbf{R_{1}}\right\}
in the environment and apply

\mathbf{F}
to
\mathbf{X_{0}}
,
which will register the
\mathbf{R_{1}.f}
fragment closure.
Since it is the result of this specific functor application, the
closure
\mathbf{R_{1}.f}
will always add
4
to its argument.
The rest of the execution proceed as shown in the previous section:
we call a new fragment, which triggers the client-side addition
2+4

and use an injection to pass the results around.

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}\ b=\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{\mathtt{Dyn}.f}\ (\mathtt{int}^{s}\ Y.a);

\displaystyle\mathtt{end};

\displaystyle\mathtt{module}\ Z=F(X);

\displaystyle\mathtt{end}\ (){};

\displaystyle\mathtt{injection}\ \mathbf{v_{1}}\ (\mathtt{frag}^{s}\ Z.b);

(a)
Server program

\displaystyle\mathtt{bind}_{m}\ \mathbf{X_{0}}=\mathtt{struct}

\displaystyle\mathtt{end};

\displaystyle\mathtt{bind}_{m}\ \mathbf{F}(Y:\mathcal{M}_{c})=\mathtt{struct}

\displaystyle\mathtt{end};

\displaystyle\mathtt{exec}\ (){}

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=(\mathtt{frag}^{c}\ \mathbf{v_{1}});

(b)
Client program

5.1.5.
Type system rules

\frac{\forall i,\ \Gamma\operatorname{\triangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}e_{i}\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathtt{serial}}}{\Gamma\operatorname{\triangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{p}}\ e_{1}\ \dots\ e_{n}\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathtt{frag}}}\quad\textsc{Fragment}\frac{\Gamma\operatorname{\triangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}e\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathtt{serial}}}{\Gamma\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}\mathtt{injection}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{v}}\ e\operatorname{:}\varepsilon}\quad\textsc{Injection}\frac{}{\Gamma\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}\mathtt{end}\ (){}\operatorname{:}\varepsilon}\quad\textsc{End}\frac{\forall i,\ \Gamma\operatorname{\triangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}p_{i}\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathcal{M}_{i}}}{\Gamma\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}\mathtt{module}\ \mathtt{Dyn}=\mathtt{fragment}_{m}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{p}}\ p_{1}.\mathtt{Dyn}\ \dots\ p_{n}.\mathtt{Dyn}\operatorname{:}\varepsilon}\quad\textsc{Fragment${}_{m}$}

Figure 40
.
Typing rules for
ML
s

\frac{\Gamma\operatorname{\triangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}e\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\tau}}{\Gamma\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{p}}=e\operatorname{:}\varepsilon}\quad\textsc{Bind}\hfill\frac{\Gamma\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}M\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}}{\Gamma\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\mathtt{bind}_{m}\ \mathbf{p}=M\operatorname{:}\varepsilon}\quad\textsc{Bind${}_{m}$}\hfill\frac{}{\Gamma\operatorname{\triangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\mathbf{x}\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathtt{serial}}}\quad\textsc{Reference}\hfill\frac{}{\Gamma\operatorname{\blacktriangleright}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\mathtt{exec}\ (){}\operatorname{:}\varepsilon}\quad\textsc{Exec}

Figure 41
.
Typing rules for
ML
s

The
ML
s
and
ML
c
typing rules are presented in

Figures

41
and
41
as
a small extension over the
ML
typing rules presented in
Section

A.2
.
Note that the typing rules for the new primitives are weakly typed
and are certainly not sound with respect to serialization and
deserialization. Given arbitrary
ML
s
and
ML
c
programs, there is no
guarantee that (de)serialization will not fail at runtime. This is on purpose.
Indeed, all these guarantees are provided by
Eliom
itself.
ML
s
and
ML
c
are target languages that are very liberal by design, so that all patterns
permitted by
Eliom
are expressible with them.
Furthermore, from an implementation perspective.
ML
s
and
ML
c
are simply

OCaml
libraries and do not rely on further compiler support.
Note that
\mathtt{Dyn}
fields are not reflected in signatures.
The fragment
Fragment
m
rule
does not enforce that the
\mathtt{Dyn}
field is present in all the arguments.
This is enforced by construction during compilation.

5.1.6.
Semantics rules

We define two reduction relations as extensions of the
ML
reduction
rules (see
Section

A.3
).
The
\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}
reduction for
ML
s
server programs is presented
in
Figure

43
.
The
\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}
reduction for
ML
c
client programs is presented
in
Figure

43
.
Let us consider a server structure
S_{s}
and a client structure
S_{c}
.
A paired
execution of the two structures is presented below:

\displaystyle S_{s}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{s}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}V_{s},\xi,\zeta,\theta_{s}

\displaystyle S_{c},\xi\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{c}\operatorname{|}\gamma\cup\zeta\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}V_{c},\xi^{\prime},\theta_{c}

Let us now detail these executions rules.
As with the
ML
reduction,

\rho_{s}
and
\rho_{c}
are
the local environments of values while
\theta_{s}
and
\theta_{c}
are
the traces for server and client executions respectively.
V_{s}
and

V_{c}
are the returned values.
Similarly to the interpreted semantics for
Eliom
ε
, the client reduction uses
global environments noted
\gamma
.

As we saw in the previous examples, server
executions emits two sets of information during execution: a queue of fragments
and a map of injections.
Mapping of injection is a traditional (global) environment where bindings are
noted
\left\{\mathbf{v}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\dots\right\}
.
The queue of fragments is noted
\xi
and
contains end tokens
\mathtt{end}
and fragment calls

\left\{\mathbf{r}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\mathbf{f}\ v_{1}\dots v_{n}\right\}
. Concatenation of fragment queues is
noted
\operatorname{\!+\!\!+\!}
. We now see the various rules in more details.

Injections

Injection bindings are collected on the server
through the
Injection
rule.
When creating a new injection binding, we inject the server-side value
using the injection of value operator, noted
\operatorname{\downarrow\!}v
and presented
in
Figure

34
. This models the serialization of values
before transmission from server to client by ensuring that only
base values and references are injected. Other kinds of values should be
handled using converters explicitly.

The injection environment
\zeta
forms a valid client-side
global environment. When executing the client-side program, we simply assume
that
\zeta
is included in the initial global environment
\gamma
.

Fragments and functors

On the server, fragments and functors calls are added to the queue through
the
Fragment
and
Fragment
m
rules.
In both rules, the reference of the associated closure or functor
is provided, along with a list of arguments. A fresh reference symbolizing
the fragment is generated and the call is added to the queue
\xi
.
Note that in the case of regular fragments, the arguments
are expressions which can themselves contains fragment calls.
The module rule
Fragment
m
is similar,
the main difference being that it only
accept module references as arguments of the call.

Fragment closures and functors are bound on the client through
the
Bind
and
Bind
m
rules, which simply binds a reference
to a value or a module value in the global environment
\gamma
.
Since
\mathtt{bind}
accepts references of the form
\mathtt{Dyn}.\mathbf{f}
, it must first
resolves
\mathtt{Dyn}
to the actual reference. This is done through the
Dyn

rule.

Segmented execution

\frac{e\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}v,\xi,\zeta,\theta\quad S\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}V,\xi^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}{\mathtt{injection}\ \mathbf{x}\ e;S\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ \rho\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}\mathbf{p^{\prime}}\forall i,\ e_{i}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}v_{i},\xi_{i},\zeta_{i},\theta_{i}\quad\mathbf{r}\text{ fresh}}{\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{p}\ e_{1}\ \dots\ e_{n}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}\mathbf{r},(\xi_{1}\operatorname{\!+\!\!+\!}\dots\xi_{n}\operatorname{\!+\!\!+\!}\left\{\mathbf{r}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\mathbf{p^{\prime}}\ \operatorname{\downarrow\!}v_{1}\dots\operatorname{\downarrow\!}v_{n}\right\}),\cup_{i}\zeta_{i},\operatorname{@}_{i}\ \theta_{i}}\quad\textsc{Fragment}\frac{\mathbf{p}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\{\},\theta\operatorname{@}\theta^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{Frag${}_{m}$}\hfill\frac{}{\text{Inherit the }\\
\\
\text{{{ML}} rules}}\quad\textsc{ClientCode}

Figure 43
.
Semantics rules for
ML
c
–
S,\xi\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}V,\xi^{\prime},\theta

In
ML
s
and
ML
c
programs, the execution of fragments is segmented
through the use of the
\mathtt{exec}\ ()/\mathtt{end}\ ()
instructions. On the
server,
\mathtt{end}
instructions are handled through the
End
rule, which
simply adds an
\mathtt{end}
token to the execution queue
\xi
.
On the client, we use the
Exec
rule, associated to the
Frag

and
Frag
m
rules.
When
\mathtt{exec}
is called, the
Exec
rule triggers
the execution of the segment of the queue until the next
\mathtt{end}
token.
Each token
\left\{\mathbf{r}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\mathbf{f}\ v_{1}\dots v_{n}\right\}
is executed
with the
Frag
rule as
the function call
(\mathbf{f}\ v_{1}\dots v_{n})
. The result of
this function call is bound to
\mathbf{r}
in the global environment
\gamma
.
Similarly, functor calls are executed using the
Frag
m
rule.
Note that for functors we also introduce the
\mathtt{Dyn}
field in the local
environment, which allows local
\mathtt{bind}
definitions.
Once all the tokens have been executed in the considered fragment queue,
we resume the usual execution.

5.2.
Slicing

In
Section

5.1
, we presented the two target languages
ML
s
and
ML
c
.
We now present the compilation process transforming
one

Eliom
ε
program
into two distinct
ML
s
and
ML
c
programs. Before giving a more formal
description in
Section

5.3
,
we present the compilation process through three examples of increasing
complexity.

Injections and fragments

14
presents an
Eliom
ε
program
containing only simple declarations involving fragments and
injections without modules.
The
Eliom
ε
program is presented on the left, while the compiled

ML
s
and
ML
c
programs are presented on the right.
In this example, a first fragment is created. It only contains an integer and is
bound to
a
. A second fragment that uses
a
is created and bound on the server to
b
.
Finally,
b
is used on the client via an injection. The program returns
4
.

For each fragment, we emit a
\mathtt{bind}
declaration on the client.
The client expression contained
in the fragment is abstracted and transformed in a closure that is bound to a fresh
reference. The number of arguments of the closure corresponds to the number of
injections inside the fragment. Similarly to the interpreted
semantics, we use the client part of the converter on the client.
In this case,
\{\{\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \}\}
is turned
into
\lambda().1
and
\{\{\penalty\ \mathtt{frag}\mathtt{\%}a+1\penalty\ \}\}
is turned
into
\lambda v.((\mathtt{frag}^{c}\ v)+1)
.
On the server, each fragment is replaced by a call to the primitive
\mathtt{fragment}
.
The arguments of the call are the identifier of the closure and all the injections
that are contained in the fragment.
The
\mathtt{fragment}
primitive, which was presented in
Section

5.1.3
,
registers that the closure declared on the client should be executed later on.
Since all the arguments of
\mathtt{fragment}
should be of type
\mathtt{serial}
, we apply the
client and server parts of the converters at the appropriate places.
The
\mathtt{exec}
and
\mathtt{end}
primitives synchronize the execution so that the order
of side effects is preserved. When
\mathtt{exec}
is encountered, it executes queued
fragment up to an
\mathtt{end}
token which was pushed by an
\mathtt{end}
primitive.
We place an
\mathtt{exec}
/
\mathtt{end}
pair at each server section. This is enough
to ensure that client code inside server fragment and client code
in regular client declaration is executed in the expected order.

Note that injections, which occur outside of fragments,
and escaped values, which occur inside fragments,
are compiled in a very different way.
Injections have the useful property that the use site and number of injections is
completely static: we can collect all the injections on the server, independently of
the client control flow and send them to the client. This is the property that allows
us to avoid communications from the client to the server.

Eliom
ε

ML
s

ML
c

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{let}_{s}\ a=\{\{\penalty\ {1}\penalty\ \}\};\\
&\\
&\mathtt{let}_{s}\ b=\{\{\penalty\ \mathtt{frag}\mathtt{\%}a+1\penalty\ \}\};\\
&\\
&\mathtt{let}_{c}\ \mathtt{return}=\mathtt{frag}\mathtt{\%}b+2;\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{let}\ a=\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{f}_{0}}\ ();\\
&\mathtt{end}\ (){};\\
&\mathtt{let}\ b=\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{f}_{1}}\ (\mathtt{frag}^{s}\ a);\\
&\mathtt{end}\ (){};\\
&\mathtt{injection}\ \mathbf{x}\ (\mathtt{frag}^{s}\ b);\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{f_{0}}=\lambda().1;\\
&\mathtt{exec}\ (){};\\
&\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{f_{1}}=\lambda v.((\mathtt{frag}^{c}\ v)+1);\\
&\mathtt{exec}\ (){};\\
&\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=(\mathtt{frag}^{c}\ \mathbf{x})+2;\end{aligned}

List of Examples 14
Compilation of expressions

Sections and modules

We now present an example with client and server modules in

15
. The lines of the various programs
have been laid out in a way that should highlight their correspondence.

We declare a server module
X
containing a client fragment,
a client functor
F
containing an injection,
a client functor application
Z

and finally the client return value, with another injection.
The compilation of the server module
X
proceeds in the following way: on the
server, we emit a module
X
similar to the original declaration but
where fragments have been replaced by a call to the
\mathtt{fragment}
primitive.
On the client, we only emit the call to
\mathtt{bind}
, without any of the server-side
code structure.
Compilation for the rest of the code proceeds in a similar manner.

This compilation scheme is correct thanks to the following insight:
In client and server modules or functors, the special instructions
for fragments and injection can be freely lifted to the outer scope.
Indeed, the fragment closure bound in
\mathbf{f_{0}}
can only reference client
elements. Since the server
X
can only introduce server-side variables, the body
of the fragment closure is independent from the server-side code.
A similar remark can be made about the client functor
F
: the functor argument
must be on the client, hence it cannot introduce new server binding. The server
identifier that is injected must have been introduced outside of functor and
the injection can be lifted outside the functor.

Using this remark, the structure of the
ML
s
and
ML
c
programs is fairly
straightforward:
Code on the appropriate side has the same shape as in the original
Eliom
ε
program
and code on the other side only contains calls to the appropriate primitives.

Eliom
ε

ML
s

ML
c

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{module}_{s}\ X=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}_{s}\ a=\{\{\penalty\ 2\penalty\ \}\}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}_{s}\ b=4\\
&\mathtt{end};\\
\\
&\mathtt{module}_{c}\ F(Y:\mathcal{M})=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}_{c}\ a=Y.b+\mathtt{int}\mathtt{\%}X.b\\
&\mathtt{end};\\
&\mathtt{module}_{c}\ Z=\\
&\hskip 9.24994ptF(\mathtt{struct}\ \mathtt{let}_{c}\ b=2\ \mathtt{end});\\
&\mathtt{let}_{c}\ \mathtt{return}=\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{frag}\mathtt{\%}X.a+Z.a;\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{module}\ X=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}\ a=\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{f}_{0}}\ ()\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}\ b=4\\
&\mathtt{end};\\
&\mathtt{end}\ (){};\\
\\
&\mathtt{injection}\ \mathbf{x_{0}}\ (\mathtt{int}^{s}\ X.b);\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
&\mathtt{injection}\ \mathbf{x_{1}}\ (\mathtt{frag}^{s}\ X.a);\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} &\\
&\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{f_{0}}=\lambda().2;\\
\\
\\
&\mathtt{exec}\ (){};\\
&\mathtt{module}\ F(Y:\mathcal{M})=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}\ a=Y.b+(\mathtt{int}^{c}\ \mathbf{x_{0}})\\
&\mathtt{end};\\
&\mathtt{module}\ Z=\\
&\hskip 9.24994ptF(\mathtt{struct}\ \mathtt{let}\ b=2\ \mathtt{end});\\
&\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt(\mathtt{frag}^{c}\ \mathbf{x_{1}})+Z.a;\end{aligned}

List of Examples 15
Compilation of client and server modules and functors

Mixed modules

Finally,
16
presents the compilation of mixed modules.
In this example, we create a mixed structure
X
containing a server
declaration and a client declaration with an injection. We define a functor
F

that takes a module containing a client integer and use it both inside a client
fragment, and inside a client declaration. We then apply
F
to
X
and
use an injection to compute the final result of the program.

The compilation of the mixed module
X
is similar to the procedure for
programs: we compile each declaration and use the
\mathtt{injection}

primitive as needed.
Additionally, we add a
\mathtt{Dyn}
field on the server-side version of the
module. The content of the
\mathtt{Dyn}
field is determined statically
for simple structures (here, it is
\mathbf{X_{0}}
).
The client-side version of the module is first bound to
\mathbf{X_{0}}
using
the
\mathtt{bind}_{m}
primitive. We then declare
X
as a simple alias.
This alias ensures that
X
is also usable in client sections transparently.

For functors, the process is similar. One additional complexity is
that the
\mathtt{Dyn}
field should be dynamically generated.
For this purpose, we add a call to the
\mathtt{fragment}_{m}
primitive.
Each call to
\mathtt{fragment}_{m}
generates a new, fresh identifier.
We also prefix each call to
\mathtt{fragment}
by the
\mathtt{Dyn}
field.
On the client, we emit two different functors.
The first one is called
F
and contains only the client
declarations to be used inside the rest of the client code.
It is used for client-side usage of mixed functors. An example
with the interpreted semantics was presented in

Section

3.6.3
.
The other one is bound to a new reference (here
\mathbf{F_{1}}
) and contains both
client declaration, along with calls to the
\mathtt{bind}

and
\mathtt{exec}
primitives.
This function is used to perform client side effects:
when the server version of
F
is applied, a call to
\mathbf{F_{1}}
is registered
and will be executed when the client reaches the associated
\mathtt{exec}
call
(here, the last one).

Eliom
ε

ML
s

ML
c

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{module}_{m}\ X=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}_{s}\ a=2\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}_{c}\ b=4+\mathtt{int}\mathtt{\%}a\\
&\mathtt{end};\\
\\[5.69054pt]
&\mathtt{module}_{m}\ F(Y:\mathcal{M})=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}_{s}\ c=\{\{\penalty\ Y.b\penalty\ \}\}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}_{c}\ d=2*Y.b\\
&\mathtt{end};\\
\\
\\
\\
\\[5.69054pt]
&\mathtt{module}_{m}\ Z=F(X);\\
\\[5.69054pt]
&\mathtt{let}_{c}\ \mathtt{return}=\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{frag}\mathtt{\%}Z.c+Z.d;\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{module}\ X=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{module}\ \mathtt{Dyn}=\mathbf{X_{0}};\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}\ a=2;\ \mathtt{end}\ (){};\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{injection}\ \mathbf{x_{0}}\ (\mathtt{int}^{s}\ a);\\
&\mathtt{end};\\[5.69054pt]
&\mathtt{module}\ F(Y:\mathcal{M})=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{module}\ \mathtt{Dyn}=\\
&\hskip 18.49988pt\mathtt{fragment}_{m}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{F_{1}}}\ (Y.\mathtt{Dyn});\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}\ c=\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{\mathtt{Dyn}.\mathbf{f}_{0}}\ ();\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{end}\ (){};\\
&\mathtt{end};\\
\\
\\[5.69054pt]
&\mathtt{module}\ Z=F(X);\\
\ &\mathtt{end}\ (){};\\
\\[5.69054pt]
&\mathtt{injection}\ \mathbf{x_{1}}\ (\mathtt{frag}^{s}\ Z.c);\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{bind}_{m}\ \mathbf{X_{0}}=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{exec}\ (){};\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}\ b=4+(\mathtt{int}^{c}\ \mathbf{x_{0}})\\
&\mathtt{end};\\
&\mathtt{module}\ X=\mathbf{X_{0}};\\[5.69054pt]
&\mathtt{bind}_{m}\ \mathbf{F_{1}}(Y:\mathcal{M})=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{bind}_{m}\ \mathtt{Dyn}.\mathbf{f}_{0}=\lambda().(Y.b);\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{exec}\ (){};\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}\ d=2*Y.b\\
&\mathtt{end};\\
&\mathtt{module}\ F(Y:\mathcal{M})=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{let}\ d=2*Y.b\\
&\mathtt{end};\\[5.69054pt]
&\mathtt{module}\ Z=F(X);\\
&\mathtt{exec}\ (){}\\[5.69054pt]
&\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt(\mathtt{frag}^{c}\ \mathbf{x_{1}})+Z.d;\end{aligned}
