A.3.
Semantics

We now define the semantics of our
ML
language. We use a rule-based
big step semantics with traces. Traces allows us to reason
about execution order in a way that is compatible with modules, as
we will see in
Section

A.3.1
.

We note
v
for values in the expression language and
V
for values
in the module language. Values are defined in
Figure

61
.
Values in the expression language can be either constants or lambdas.
Module values are either structures, which are list of bindings of
values, or functors.
We note
\rho
the execution environment. Execution environments
are a list of value bindings. Note here that the execution environment
is not mutable, since reference cells are not in the language. We
note the concatenation of environment
+
. Environment access is noted

\rho(x)=v
where
x
has value
v
in
\rho
.
The same notation is also used for structures.
Traces are lists of messages. For now, we consider messages
that are values and are emitted with a
\mathtt{print}
operation.
The empty trace is noted
\langle\rangle
. Concatenation of traces is noted
\operatorname{@}
.

Given an expression
e
(resp. a module
m
), an execution
environment
\rho
, a value
v
(resp.
V
) and a trace
\theta
,

e\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}v{},\theta

means that
e
reduces to
v
in
\rho
and prints
\theta
.
The reduction rules are given in
Figure

61
.
The rules for the expression language are fairly traditional.
Variables and paths must be resolved using the
Var
and
QualVar

rules. Applications are done in two steps: first, we reduce both
the function and the argument with the
App
rule,
then we apply the appropriate reduction
rule for the application:
Beta
for lambda expressions,
Y
for
fixpoints and
Delta
for constants.
The
\delta
operation gives meaning to application of a constant
to a value.
\delta(c,v)=v^{\prime},\theta
means that
c
applied to
v

returns
v^{\prime}
and emits the trace
\theta
. Let bindings
are treated in a similar manner than lambda expressions: the left
hand side is executed, added to the environment, then the right hand side
is executed.

The module language has similar rules for identifiers and application.
In this case, the
Beta
and
App
rule have been combined in

ModBeta
. Additional rules for declarations are also present.
Type declarations are ignored (
TypeDecl
). Values and module
declarations (
ValDecl
and
ModDecl
) are treated similarly
to let bindings: the body of the binding is executed, added to the environment
and then the rest of the structure is executed.

A.3.1.
Traces and Printing

Traces allow us to visualize the execution order of programs. In
particular, if we prove that code transformation preserves traces,
it ensures that the execution order is preserved.
Traces allow us to reason about execution without introducing
references and other side-effecting operations in our language, which
would make the presentation significantly more complex.

One example of operation using traces is the
\mathtt{print}
constant.
Typing and semantics of
\mathtt{print}
are provided in
Figure

58
.

\mathtt{print}
accepts any value, prints it, and returns it. From a typing
point of view,
\mathtt{print}
has the same type as the identity: a polymorphic function
which returns its input. We make use of the fact
that the
Const
typing rule also uses the instanciation for type schemes.
The semantics of
\mathtt{print}
is provided via the
Delta
rule: it returns its argument
directly but also emits a trace containing the given argument.

\frac{}{\text{TypeOf}(\mathtt{print})=\forall\alpha.(\mathrm{\alpha}\operatorname{\rightarrow}\mathrm{\alpha})}\quad\textsc{PrintTy}\frac{}{\delta(\mathtt{print},v)=v,\langle v\rangle}\quad\textsc{PrintExec}

Figure 58
.
Typing and execution rules for
\mathtt{print}

We now present an example using
\mathtt{print}
.
We assume the existence of the type

\mathtt{int}
, a set of constant corresponding to the integers
and an associated operation
+
.
We wish to type and execute the expression
e
defined as
\mathtt{let}\ x=(\mathtt{print}\ 3)\ \mathtt{in}\ (\mathtt{print}\ (x+1))

Let us first show that
e
is of type
\mathtt{int}
. The type derivation is provided
in
. The typing derivation is fairly direct:
we use the
Const
rule to type
\mathtt{print}
as
\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}\operatorname{\rightarrow}\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}

and apply it to integers with the rule
App
.
We can now look at the execution of
e
, which returns
4

with a trace
\langle 3;4\rangle
. The execution derivation is shown in

. The first step is to decompose the let-binding.
We first reduce
(\mathtt{print}\ 3)
, which can be directly done with
the
Delta
rule. This gives us
3
with a trace
\langle 3\rangle
.
We then reduce
(\mathtt{print}\ (x+1))
in
the environment where
x
is associated to
3
.
Before resolving the application of
\mathtt{print}
with the
Delta
rule,
we need to reduce its argument with the
App
rule.
We obtain
4
with a trace
\langle 4\rangle
.
We return the result of the right hand side of the left and the concatenation
of both traces by usage of the
LetIn
rule,
which gives us
4
with a trace
\langle 3;4\rangle
.

\frac{\frac{\frac{\text{TypeOf}(\mathtt{print})\operatorname{\succ}\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}\operatorname{\rightarrow}\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}}{\operatorname{\triangleright}\mathtt{print}\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}\operatorname{\rightarrow}\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Left=Const}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\text{TypeOf}(3)=\mathtt{int}}{\operatorname{\triangleright}3\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}}}{\operatorname{\triangleright}(\mathtt{print}\ 3)\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}}\quad\textsc{Left=App}\quad\frac{\frac{\vdots}{\operatorname{\triangleright}\mathtt{print}\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}\operatorname{\rightarrow}\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\vdots}{\operatorname{\triangleright}x+1\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}}}{(\mathtt{val}\ x:\mathtt{int})\operatorname{\triangleright}(\mathtt{print}\ (x+1))\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}}\quad\textsc{Right=App}}{\operatorname{\triangleright}\mathtt{let}\ x=(\mathtt{print}\ 3)\ \mathtt{in}\ (\mathtt{print}\ (x+1))\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}}\quad\textsc{Left=LetIn, Right=LetIn}

e
–
\operatorname{\triangleright}e\operatorname{:}\mathrm{\mathtt{int}}

A.3.2.
Modules

We now present an example of reduction involving modules. Our example
program
P
is presented in
. It consists of
two declarations: a module declaration
X
which contains a single
declaration
a
, and the return value of the program, which is equal to

X.a
. It is fairly easy to see that the program
P
return a value
of type
\mathtt{int}
, hence we focus on the execution of
P
, which
is presented in
.
The derivation is slightly simplified for clarity. In particular, rules
such as
EmptyStruct
are elided.
The first step is to apply the
Program
and
ModuleDecl

rules in order to execute the content of each declaration.
The declaration of
X
, on the left side, can be reduced by
first applying the
Struct
rule in order to extract the
content of the module structure, then
ValDecl
, to reduce
the declaration of
a
. These reductions give us the structure
value
\left\{a\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}
.
We now execute the declaration of
\mathtt{return}
. According to the
ModuleDecl

rule, we must do so in a new environment containing
X
:
\left\{X\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\left\{a\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}\right\}
.
In order to reduce
X.a
, we must use the
QualModVar
rule, which reduces
qualified variables. This means we first reduce
X
, which according
to the environment gives us
\left\{a\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}
, noted
V
.
We then look up
a
in
V
, which returns
3
.
To return, we first compose the resulting structure value from both
declaration:
\left\{X\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\left\{a\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}\right\}+\left\{\mathtt{return}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}
.
We then lookup
\mathtt{return}
in this structure, which gives us
3
.

\displaystyle\mathtt{prog}

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\mathtt{module}\ X=\mathtt{struct}\ \mathtt{let}\ a=3\ \mathtt{end}

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=X.a

\displaystyle\mathtt{end}

(a)

The program
P

ex]ml:mod:ex

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{}{3\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}3{},\langle\rangle}}{\mathtt{let}\ a=3\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}\left\{a\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}{},\langle\rangle}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Left=ValDecl}}{\left(\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{struct}\quad&\kern 5.0pt\mathtt{let}\ a=3\quad&\mathtt{end}\quad\end{aligned}\right)\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}\left\{a\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}{},\langle\rangle}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Left=Struct}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\frac{\frac{\rho(X)=V}{X\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\ }}V\equiv\left\{a\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}{},\langle\rangle}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Left=ModVar}\hskip 7.3612ptV(a)=3}{X.a\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \left\{X\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\left\{a\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}\right\}\ }}3{},\langle\rangle}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{Right=QualModVar}}{\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=X.a\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \left\{X\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\left\{a\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}\right\}\ }}\left\{\mathtt{return}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}{},\langle\rangle}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right=ValDecl}}{\left(\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{module}\ X=\mathtt{struct}\ \mathtt{let}\ a=3\ \mathtt{end}\quad&\mathtt{let}\ \mathtt{return}=X.a\end{aligned}\right)\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}\left\{X\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\left\{a\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}\right\}+\left\{\mathtt{return}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}3\right\}{},\langle\rangle}\quad\textsc{Left=ModuleDecl,Right=ModuleDecl}}{P\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}3{},\langle\rangle}\quad\textsc{Left=Program,Right=Program}

(b)

Execution of
P

A.3.3.
Notes on Soundness

Soundness properties, which correspond to the often misquoted
“Well typed programs cannot go wrong.”, have been proven for many variants
of the
ML
language.
Unfortunately, stating and proving the soundness property for big step semantics
and
ML
modules requires a fairly large amount of machinery which
we do not attempt to provide here.
Instead, we give pointers to various relevant work containing such proofs.

Soundness for a small step semantics of our expression language is
provided by
Wright and
Felleisen 1994
.
At a larger scale,
Owens 2008
proves
the soundness of a small step semantics for a very large portion of the

OCaml
expression language using the Locally Nameless Coq framework

(
Aydemir et al. 2008
)
.
Soundness of a big step semantics has been proved and mechanized for several
richer languages

(
Amin and Rompf 2017
;
Tofte 1988
;
Owens
et al. 2016
;
Lee
et al. 2007
;
Garrigue 2009
)
.

Unfortunately, as far as we are aware, soundness of Leroy’s module language
with higher order applicative functors has not be proved directly and is a fairly
delicate subject.
The most recent work of interest is
(
Rossberg
et al. 2014
)
, which presents
an elaboration scheme from
ML
modules, including applicative
OCaml
-style
modules, into System
F_{\omega}
. Soundness then relies on soundness of
the elaboration (provided in the article) and soundness of System
F_{\omega}
.
In this work, the applicative/generative behavior of functors is decided
depending on its purity, which is much more precise than what
is done in
OCaml
.

Appendix B
Semantics preservation

We now prove
Theorem

3
which states that
given an
Eliom
ε
program
P
, the trace of its execution is the same
as the concatenation of the traces of
\left\langle P\right\rangle_{s}
and

\left\langle P\right\rangle_{c}
. More formally:

Theorem 1 (Compilation preserves semantics).

Given sets of constants where converters are well-behaved,
given an
Eliom
ε
program
P
respecting the slicability hypothesis and
such that

P\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \{\}\ }}v,\theta

then

\displaystyle\left\langle P\right\rangle_{s}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \{\}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}(),\xi,\zeta,\theta_{s}

\displaystyle\left\langle P\right\rangle_{c},\xi\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \{\}\operatorname{|}\zeta\rightarrow\gamma\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}v,\xi^{\prime},\theta_{c}

\displaystyle\theta=\theta_{s}\operatorname{@}\theta_{c}

B.1.
Hoisting

In
Section

5.2
, we mentioned that a useful property
of injections and fragments is that they can be partially lifted
outside sections.
This property can be used to simplify the simulation proofs.
We consider the code transformation that hoists the content of injections
out of fragments, client declarations and mixed functors in a way
that preserve semantics. This transformation can be decomposed
in two parts.

Injections

We decompose injections inside fragments and client declarations into simpler
components.
For example, the
Eliom
ε
piece of code presented in
Figure

62(a)

is decomposed in
Figure

62(b)
by moving out the application
of the converter and leaving only a call to the
\mathtt{serial}
converter.
All injections using a converter than is not
\mathtt{serial}
nor
\mathtt{frag}

can be decomposed in such a way.

Since injections
can only be used on variables or constants and that no server bindings can be
introduced inside a fragment, scoping is preserved.
Furthermore, by definition of converters and their client and server
components, this transformation preserves typing.
It also preserves the dynamic semantics as long as the order
of hoisting correspond to the order of evaluation. This can be
seen by inspecting the reduction relation for server code under client
contexts
\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{c/s}
.
Finally, it trivially preserves the semantics of the compiled program since
it corresponds exactly to how converters are decomposed during compilation.

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}\ a=1+2\ \mathtt{in}\

\displaystyle\{\{\penalty\ 3+\mathtt{int}\mathtt{\%}a\penalty\ \}\}

(a)

Fragment with injections

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}\ a=1+2\ \mathtt{in}\

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}\ a^{\prime}=(\mathtt{int}^{s}\ a)\ \mathtt{in}\

\displaystyle\{\{\penalty\ 3+(\mathtt{int}^{c}\ \mathtt{serial}\mathtt{\%}a^{\prime})\penalty\ \}\}

(b)

Fragment with hoisted injections

List of Examples 24

Hoisting on fragments

This allows us to assume that reduction of server code in client context
only uses variable lookup and never leads to any evaluation.
In particular, this will avoid having to deal with the case
of fragments being executed inside the reduction of another fragment (to
see why this could happen, consider the case of a converter
of type

\forall\alpha_{c}.(\mathrm{unit}\operatorname{\rightarrow}\mathrm{\{\mathrm{\alpha_{c}}\}})\operatorname{\leadsto}\alpha_{c}
).

In the rest of this section, we assume that reductions of the
Eliom
ε
rule

Fragment
are always of the following shape:

\frac{e\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{c}\ }}_{c/s}\overline{e},\varepsilon,\langle\rangle}{\{\{\penalty\ e\penalty\ \}\}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{s}\ }}_{s}\mathbf{r},(\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{r}=\overline{e}),\langle\rangle}\frac{}{\text{ where }\overline{e}=e\!\left[f_{i}\mathtt{\%}x_{i}\mapsto\rho_{s}(x_{i})\right]_{i}\text{ and }f_{i}\in\{\mathtt{serial},\mathtt{frag}\}}

and that reductions of the
Eliom
ε
rule
ClientDecl

are always of the following shape:

\frac{}{D_{c}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{s}\ }}_{c/s}\overline{D_{c}},\varepsilon,\langle\rangle}\frac{}{\text{ where }\overline{D_{c}}=D_{c}\!\left[f_{i}\mathtt{\%}x_{i}\mapsto\rho_{s}(x_{i})\right]_{i}\text{ and }f_{i}\in\{\mathtt{serial},\mathtt{frag}\}}

Injections inside mixed modules

We also hoist injections completely out of mixed contexts to the outer
englobing scope.
For example in the functor presented in
Figure

63(a)
,
we can lift the injection out of the functor, as show in

Figure

63(b)
.
This is valid since injections can only reference
content outside of the functor, by typing.
Semantics is similarly preserved since injections inside functors
are reduced immediately when encountering a functor, as per rule

ModClosure
in
Figure

35
.

This allows us to assume that the reduction of a mixed module
will never lead to the reduction of an injection.

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}_{s}\ x=\dots

\displaystyle\mathtt{module}_{m}\ F{(X:\mathcal{M})}=\mathtt{struct}

\displaystyle\quad\mathtt{let}_{c}\ y=f\mathtt{\%}x

\displaystyle\mathtt{end}

(a)

Mixed functor with injections

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}_{s}\ x=\dots

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}_{c}\ y^{\prime}=f\mathtt{\%}x

\displaystyle\mathtt{module}_{m}\ F{(X:\mathcal{M})}=\mathtt{struct}

\displaystyle\quad\mathtt{let}_{c}\ y=y^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathtt{end}

(b)

Mixed functor with hoisted injections

Hoisting on mixed modules

B.2.
Preliminaries

Let us start with some naming conventions.
Identifiers with a hat, such
as
\widehat{\gamma}
, are related to the compiled semantics. For example,
while the server environment for the interpreted semantics
is noted
\rho_{s}
, the environment for the execution of the target
language
ML
s
is noted
\widehat{\rho}_{s}
.
This naming convention is only for ease of reading and does not apply
a formal relation between the objects with and without hats, unless
indicated explicitly.

B.2.1.
Remarks about global environments

Let us make some preliminary remarks about global environments in the
Eliom
ε
client generated programs and in
ML
c
.

Given a global environment
\gamma
resulting of
\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \ }}_{c}
,
it contains only two kinds of references:

•

Closure fragments, noted
\mathbf{f}
, which come from the
execution of
\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}
.
The associated value is always a environment (
i.e.
, a signature).

•

Fragment values, noted
\mathbf{r}
, which come from the execution of

\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{with}
.

In the rest of this section, we consider that we can always
decompose global environments
\gamma
in two parts:
a fragment value environment
\gamma_{r}
containing all the references

\mathbf{r}
that were produced by
\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{with}
and
a fragment closure environment
\gamma_{f}
containing
only binding of the form
\left\{\mathbf{f}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\rho\right\}
that were produced by

\mathtt{bind}\ \mathtt{env}
.

Similarly, given a global environment
\widehat{\gamma}
used in
ML
c
.
There are only three kind of references:

•

Closure fragments, noted
\mathbf{f}
, which come from the slicing of syntactic
fragments in the source program.
The associated value is always a closure.

•

Fragment values, noted
\mathbf{r}
come from the execution of
fragments in the fragment queue.

•

Injections, noted
\mathbf{x}
. The associated values
must be serializable, and hence
can only be references or constants in
\text{Const}_{b}
.

In the rest of this section, we consider that we can always decompose global
compiled environment
\widehat{\gamma}
into
a fragment closure environment
\widehat{\gamma}_{f}
, a fragment value environment

\widehat{\gamma}_{r}
and an injection environment
\zeta
.

B.2.2.
Client equivalence

Definition 0 (Client values equivalence).

Given
v
an
Eliom
client value,
v^{\prime}
an
ML
c
value
and
\zeta
an environment of references,

v
and
v^{\prime}
are equivalent under
\zeta
,
noted
v\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}v^{\prime}
, if and only if they are equals after
substitution by
\zeta
:
v\!\left[\zeta\right]=v^{\prime}\!\left[\zeta\right]
.

We extend this notation to environments and traces.

Definition 0 (Global environment equivalence).

We say that an
Eliom
ε
global environment

\gamma=\gamma_{f}\cup\gamma_{r}

and an
ML
c
global environment

\widehat{\gamma}=\widehat{\gamma}_{f}\cup\widehat{\gamma}_{r}\cup\zeta

are synchronized if and only if the following conditions hold.

•

The reference environments are equivalent:

\gamma_{r}\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\widehat{\gamma}_{r}

•

The domains of
\gamma_{f}
and
\widehat{\gamma}_{f}
coincides, and:

–

For each
\mathbf{f}
in these environments such that

\gamma_{f}(\mathbf{f})=\rho
and that

\widehat{\gamma}_{f}(\mathbf{f})=\lambda x_{0}\dots x_{n}.\widehat{\rho}.e
, then
the following property must hold.

We must have that
\rho\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\widehat{\rho}
and that
for all
v_{0},\dots,v_{n}
,
\widehat{v}_{0},\dots,\widehat{v}_{n}
such that
for all
i
,
v_{i}\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\widehat{v}_{i}
;
then:

\displaystyle e\!\left[x_{i}\mapsto v_{i}\right]_{i}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma\ }}_{c}v,\theta\implies(\lambda\overline{x}_{i}.\widehat{\rho}.e\ \widehat{v}_{0}\dots\widehat{v}_{n})\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \operatorname{|}\widehat{\gamma}\rightarrow\widehat{\gamma}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\widehat{v},\widehat{\theta}

with
v\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\widehat{v}
and
\theta\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\widehat{\theta}

–

For each
\mathbf{F}
in these environments such that

\gamma_{f}(\mathbf{F})=\rho
and that

\widehat{\gamma}_{f}(\mathbf{F})=\mathtt{functor}(\widehat{\rho})(Y_{i}\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i}M
, we have
\rho\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\widehat{\rho}
.

Definition 0 (Fragment closure environment).

We consider that
\widehat{\gamma}_{f}
is a fragment closure environment for
the
Eliom
ε
server expression
e_{s}
, noted
FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f},e_{s})
, if
for each
\left\{\mathbf{f}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\lambda\overline{x}_{i}.\widehat{\rho}.e^{\prime}\right\}
in

\widehat{\gamma}_{f}
, for each
\{\{\penalty\ e\penalty\ \}\}_{\mathbf{f}}
in
\operatorname{FRAGS}(e_{s})

we have
e^{\prime}=e\!\left[f_{i}\mathtt{\%}x_{i}\mapsto x_{i}\right]_{i}
.

Definition 0 (Functor closure environment).

We consider that
\widehat{\gamma}_{f}
is a functor closure environment for
the
Eliom
ε
module expression
M
, noted
FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f},M)
, if
for each
\left\{\mathbf{F}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\mathtt{functor}(\widehat{\rho})(Y_{i}\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i}\widehat{S}\right\}
in

\widehat{\gamma}_{f}
, for each
(\mathtt{struct}\ S\ \mathtt{end}_{\mathbf{F}})
in
M_{s}

we have
\widehat{S}=\left\langle S\right\rangle_{c}
. Additionally, we require
that
\widehat{\gamma}_{f}
be a fragment closure environment
for each expression contained in
S
.

In the rest of this section, we use the same notation for both properties.
We extend this notation to server declarations, server values
(by looking under closures) and server environments.

Lemma 0 (Reduction up to equivalence).

Given
\rho
,
\widehat{\rho}
,

\gamma=\gamma_{f}\cup\gamma_{r}
,

\widehat{\gamma}=\widehat{\gamma}_{f}\cup\widehat{\gamma}_{r}\cup\zeta
,

e
and
\widehat{e}

such that:

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\widehat{\rho}

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\widehat{\gamma}

\displaystyle e[\zeta]

\displaystyle=\widehat{e}[\zeta]

\displaystyle e\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma\ }}_{c}v,\theta

Then we have:

\displaystyle\widehat{e}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}\operatorname{|}\widehat{\gamma}\rightarrow\widehat{\gamma}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\widehat{v},\widehat{\theta}

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\widehat{v}

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\widehat{\theta}

Proof.

The only difference between
Eliom
ε
client expressions and

ML
c
expressions are the presence of extra references for injections
in
ML
c
. Indeed, syntactic injections have been removed either
by the server execution or by compilation and
\mathtt{bind}\
constructs
are only accessible at the module level. Since we assume
that the original expression
e
and the compiled expression
\widehat{e}

are the same up to the injection environment
\zeta
, we can
trivially mimic the execution of
e
in
\widehat{e}
by induction.
∎

B.2.3.
Server equivalence

Definition 0 (Server value equivalence).

Given
v
an
Eliom
server value,
\widehat{v}
an
ML
s
value.
We say they are equivalent, noted
v\simeq^{s}\widehat{v}
if
and only if

\displaystyle v\!\left[\{\{\penalty\ e_{i}\penalty\ \}\}_{\mathbf{f}}\mapsto\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{f}}\ \overline{x_{i,j}}\right]_{i}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}

where
\overline{x_{i,j}}=\operatorname{INJS}(e_{i})

We extend this notation to environments and traces.

B.3.
Server expressions and structures

We first look at server expressions and structures.
By definition of the server reduction relation for
Eliom
ε
,
the emitted program is a series of binds.

Lemma 0 (Server expressions are simulable).

We consider an
Eliom
ε
server expression
e
;
the
Eliom
ε
environments
\rho_{s}
,
\rho_{c}
and

\gamma=\gamma_{f}\cup\gamma_{r}
;
the target environment
\widehat{\rho}_{s}
,
\widehat{\rho}_{c}
and

\widehat{\gamma}=\widehat{\gamma}_{f}\cup\widehat{\gamma}_{r}\cup\zeta
.

If the expression
e
has valid server and client executions:

\displaystyle e\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{s}\ }}_{s}v,\mu,\theta_{s}

\displaystyle\mu\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{c}\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{c}\{\},\theta_{c}

and the following invariants hold:

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}_{c}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\rho_{c}

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}_{s}

\displaystyle\simeq^{s}\rho_{s}

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}\gamma

\displaystyle FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f},e)

\displaystyle FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f},\rho_{s})

Then
\widehat{e}=e\!\left[\{\{\penalty\ e_{i}\penalty\ \}\}_{\mathbf{f}}\mapsto\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{f}}\ \overline{x_{i,j}}\right]_{i}
has an equivalent execution.

\frac{}{\widehat{e}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{s}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}\widehat{v},\xi_{\bullet},\{\},\widehat{\theta}_{s}}\frac{}{\mathtt{exec}\ (),\xi_{\bullet}\operatorname{\!+\!\!+\!}\mathtt{end}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{c}\operatorname{|}\widehat{\gamma}\rightarrow\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\varepsilon,[\ ],\widehat{\theta}_{c}}

with the following invariants:

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f}^{\prime},v)

\displaystyle\widehat{v}

\displaystyle\simeq^{s}v

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta}_{s}

\displaystyle\simeq^{s}\theta_{s}

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta}_{c}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\theta_{c}

Proof.

We consider an expression
e
;
the
Eliom
ε
environments
\rho_{s}
,
\rho_{c}
and

\gamma=\gamma_{f}\cup\gamma_{r}
;
the target environment
\widehat{\rho}_{s}
,
\widehat{\rho}_{c}
and

\widehat{\gamma}=\widehat{\gamma}_{f}\cup\widehat{\gamma}_{r}\cup\zeta
.
such that

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}\gamma

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}_{c}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\rho_{c}

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}_{s}

\displaystyle\simeq^{s}\rho_{s}

\displaystyle FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f},e)

\displaystyle FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f},\rho_{s})

We will proceed by induction over the executions of
e
and
\mu
.
The only case of interest is when the server expression is a fragment.

\bullet
Case
\{\{\penalty\ e\penalty\ \}\}_{\mathbf{f}}
.

We assume that the following executions hold:

\frac{\rho_{s}(x_{i})=v_{i}}{\{\{\penalty\ e\penalty\ \}\}_{\mathbf{f}}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{s}\ }}_{m}\mathbf{r},\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{r}=\overline{e}\ \texttt{with}\ \mathbf{f},\langle\rangle}\frac{\gamma(\mathbf{f})=\rho\quad\overline{e}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma\ }}_{c}v_{c},\theta_{c}}{(\mathtt{bind}\ \mathbf{r}=\overline{e}\ \texttt{with}\ \mathbf{f})\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{c}\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{c}\{\},\theta_{c}}

where

\overline{e}=e\!\left[f_{i}\mathtt{\%}x_{i}\mapsto\operatorname{\downarrow\!}v_{i}\right]_{i}
and

\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma\cup\left\{\mathbf{r}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}v_{c}\right\}
.
We have
\widehat{e}
equal to
\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{f}}\ x_{1}\dots x_{n}
.

We first consider the execution of
\widehat{e}
.
We can easily
construct the following execution.

\frac{\widehat{\rho}_{s}(x_{i})=\widehat{v}_{i}}{\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{f}}\ x_{1}\dots x_{n}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{s}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}\mathbf{r},\left\{\mathbf{r}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\mathbf{f}\ \operatorname{\downarrow\!}\widehat{v}_{1}\dots\operatorname{\downarrow\!}\widehat{v}_{n}\right\},\{\},\langle\rangle}

By hypothesis, for each
i
,
v_{i}\simeq^{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}\widehat{v}_{i}
.
This gives us that

\operatorname{\downarrow\!}v_{i}\simeq^{c}_{\widehat{\gamma}}\operatorname{\downarrow\!}\widehat{v}_{i}
.
We trivially have that

\mathbf{r}\simeq^{s}_{\widehat{\gamma}}\mathbf{r}

Let us now look at the client execution.
By client execution of
\mu
,
\gamma(\mathbf{f})=\rho
.
Since
\gamma\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\widehat{\gamma}
, we have

\left\{\mathbf{f}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\lambda x_{0}\dots x_{n}.\widehat{\rho}.e^{\prime}\right\}\in\widehat{\gamma}

and
\rho\simeq^{c}_{\widehat{\gamma}}\widehat{\rho}
.
Furthermore, since
FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f},\{\{\penalty\ e\penalty\ \}\}_{\mathbf{f}})
, we
know that that
e^{\prime}=e\!\left[f_{i}\mathtt{\%}x_{i}\mapsto x_{i}\right]_{i}
.
We have by hypothesis that

\overline{e}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{c}\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma\ }}_{c}v_{c},\theta_{c}
.
Since
\overline{e}=e^{\prime}\!\left[x_{i}\mapsto\operatorname{\downarrow\!}v_{i}\right]

and since for all
i
,
\operatorname{\downarrow\!}v_{i}\simeq^{c}_{\widehat{\gamma}}\operatorname{\downarrow\!}\widehat{v}_{i}
,
we can use
Lemma

6
to build the following reduction:

\frac{\frac{\frac{e^{\prime}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{c}\cup\left\{x_{i}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\widehat{v}_{i}\right\}_{i}\operatorname{|}\widehat{\gamma}\rightarrow\widehat{\gamma}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\widehat{v},\widehat{\theta}_{c}}{\lambda x_{1}\dots x_{n}.\widehat{\rho}.e^{\prime}\ \operatorname{\downarrow\!}\widehat{v}_{1}\dots\operatorname{\downarrow\!}\widehat{v}_{n}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{c}\operatorname{|}\widehat{\gamma}\rightarrow\widehat{\gamma}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\widehat{v},\widehat{\theta}_{c}}}{\mathbf{f}\ \operatorname{\downarrow\!}\widehat{v}_{1}\dots\operatorname{\downarrow\!}\widehat{v}_{n}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{c}\operatorname{|}\widehat{\gamma}\rightarrow\widehat{\gamma}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\widehat{v},\widehat{\theta}_{c}}}{\mathtt{exec}\ (),\left\{\mathbf{r}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\mathbf{f}\ \operatorname{\downarrow\!}\widehat{v}_{1}\dots\operatorname{\downarrow\!}\widehat{v}_{n}\right\}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{c}\operatorname{|}\widehat{\gamma}\rightarrow\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\varepsilon,[\ ],\widehat{\theta}_{c}}

Where
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}=\widehat{\gamma}\cup\left\{\mathbf{r}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\widehat{v}\right\}
.
By
Lemma

6
, we have
that
v\simeq^{c}_{\widehat{\gamma}}\widehat{v}
and

\theta_{c}\simeq^{c}_{\widehat{\gamma}}\widehat{\theta}_{c}
.
The only part that is changed in
\gamma^{\prime}
and
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
is
the fragment reference environment, hence we easily have that

\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}\simeq^{c}\gamma^{\prime}
, which concludes.
∎

\bullet
Other cases.

In other cases, we first note that references manipulated
inside server code can only fragment references
\mathbf{r}
.
By hypothesis, the same references are considered before and
after compilation. Since the fragment closure environment
hypothesis ranges over all server expressions, including the one
in closures, it is easy to preserve it during execution.
The rest is a very simple induction.
∎

∎

If the expression

has valid server and client executions:

\displaystyle D\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{s}\ }}_{s}V,\mu,\theta_{s}

\displaystyle\mu\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{c}\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{c}\{\},\theta_{c}

and the following invariants hold:

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}_{\zeta}\rho_{c}

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}_{s}

\displaystyle\simeq^{s}\rho_{s}

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}\gamma

\displaystyle FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f},D)

\displaystyle FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f},\rho_{s})

Then
\widehat{D}=D\!\left[\{\{\penalty\ e_{i}\penalty\ \}\}_{\mathbf{f}}\mapsto\mathtt{fragment}\ \mathbf{\mathbf{f}}\ \overline{x_{i,j}}\right]_{i}
have an equivalent execution.

\frac{}{\widehat{D}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{s}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}\widehat{V},\xi_{\bullet},\{\},\widehat{\theta}_{s}}\frac{}{\mathtt{exec}\ (),\xi_{\bullet}\operatorname{\!+\!\!+\!}\mathtt{end}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{c}\operatorname{|}\widehat{\gamma}\rightarrow\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\varepsilon,[\ ],\widehat{\theta}_{c}}

Lemma 0 (Structures are simulable).

We consider a slicable structure
S
;
the
Eliom
ε
environments
\rho_{s}
,
\rho_{c}
and

,
\widehat{\rho}_{c}
and

\widehat{\gamma}=\widehat{\gamma}_{f}\cup\widehat{\gamma}_{r}\cup\zeta
.

\displaystyle S\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{s}\ }}_{m}V_{s},\mu,\theta_{s}

\displaystyle\mu\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{c}\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{c}V_{c},\theta_{c}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}\gamma

\displaystyle FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f},S)

\displaystyle FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f},\rho_{s})

then for any
\xi
,
the compiled structures have equivalent executions

\displaystyle\left\langle S\right\rangle_{s}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{s}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}\widehat{V}_{s},\xi_{\bullet},\zeta_{\bullet},\widehat{\theta}_{s}

\displaystyle\left\langle S\right\rangle_{c},\xi_{\bullet}\operatorname{\!+\!\!+\!}\xi\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{c}\operatorname{|}\zeta_{\bullet}\cup\widehat{\gamma}\rightarrow\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\widehat{V}_{c},\xi^{\prime},\widehat{\theta}_{c}

with the following invariants:

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{s}

\displaystyle\simeq^{s}V_{s}

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta}_{s}

\displaystyle\simeq^{s}\theta_{s}

\displaystyle FCE(

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}_{f}^{\prime},V_{s})

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{c}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}_{\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}}V_{c}

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta}_{c}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}_{\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}}\theta_{c}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}\gamma

\displaystyle FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f},\rho_{s})

We will now proceed by induction over the execution of

Case

– Base declaration.

We assume that the following executions hold:

\frac{D_{b}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{s}\ }}_{b}V_{s},\varepsilon,\theta_{s}\quad S^{\prime}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{s}+V_{s}\ }}_{m}V^{\prime}_{s},\mu,\theta^{\prime}_{s}}{D_{b};S^{\prime}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{s}\ }}_{m}V_{s}+V^{\prime}_{s},(D_{b};\mu),\theta_{s}\operatorname{@}\theta^{\prime}_{s}}\frac{D_{b}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{c}\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma\ }}_{c}V_{c},\theta_{c}\quad\mu\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{c}+V_{c}\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{c}V^{\prime}_{c},\theta^{\prime}_{c}}{D_{b};\mu\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \rho_{c}\operatorname{|}\gamma\rightarrow\gamma^{\prime}\ }}_{c}V_{c}+V^{\prime}_{c},\theta_{c}\operatorname{@}\theta^{\prime}_{c}}

By definition, base fragments can’t be present, hence

\left\langle D_{b}\right\rangle_{s}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{functor}(\rho_{s})(X_{i}\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i}\mathtt{struct}\ S\ \mathtt{end}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{functor}(\rho_{c})(X_{i}\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i}\mathtt{struct}\ S\rvert_{c}\ \mathtt{end}

We recall that by hoisting, the body of the functors contains

We have the following compilations:

\displaystyle\left\langle\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{module}_{m}\ F(X_{i}:\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i}=\mathtt{struct}\\

We trivially have the following execution:

\frac{}{\left\langle\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{module}_{m}\ F(X_{i}:\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i}=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\quad S\\
&\mathtt{end}_{\mathbf{F}}\end{aligned}\right\rangle_{s}\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{s}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{s}}}}\left\{F\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\widehat{V}_{s}\right\},\xi_{\bullet},\{\},\langle\rangle}\frac{}{\left\langle\begin{aligned} &\mathtt{module}_{m}\ F(X_{i}:\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i}=\mathtt{struct}\\
&\quad S\\
&\mathtt{end}_{\mathbf{F}}\end{aligned}\right\rangle_{c},\xi\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \widehat{\rho}_{c}\operatorname{|}\widehat{\gamma}\rightarrow\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}\ }}_{{\textsc{ML\textsubscript{c}}}}\left\{F\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\widehat{V}_{c}\right\},\xi,\langle\rangle}

Where
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma\cup\left\{\mathbf{F}\operatorname{\!\mapsto\!}\widehat{V}_{\mathbf{F}}\right\}
with the following values:

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{s}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{functor}(\widehat{\rho}_{s})(X_{i}\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i}\mathtt{struct}\ \left\langle S\right\rangle_{s}\ \mathtt{end}

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{c}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{functor}(\widehat{\rho}_{c})(X_{i}\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i}\mathtt{struct}\ S\rvert_{c}\ \mathtt{end}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{functor}(\widehat{\rho}_{c})(X_{i}\operatorname{:}\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i}\mathtt{struct}\ \left\langle S\right\rangle_{c}\ \mathtt{end}

We now need to show that the invariants still hold. We easily
have that
\widehat{V}_{c}\simeq^{c}_{\widehat{\gamma}}V_{c}
.
By definition of equivalence over mixed functors, we have

\widehat{V}_{s}\simeq^{s}V_{s}
. Indeed, the body of the functor in
\widehat{V}_{s}

is the server compilation of the body of the mixed
functor
V_{s}
and the captured environments corresponds.
Finally, we have that the body of
\widehat{V}_{\mathbf{F}}
is the
client compilation of the body of the mixed functors and that the
capture environment corresponds to
\gamma^{\prime}(\mathbf{F})
. Thus
we get that
FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f}^{\prime},V_{s})
.
By definition of the annotation function, the reference
\mathbf{F}
could not
have appeared on a previously executed structure,
hence we still have that
FCE(\widehat{\gamma}_{f}^{\prime},\widehat{\rho}_{s})
.

Hence, all the following invariants are respected, which concludes.

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{s}

\displaystyle\simeq^{s}V_{s}

\displaystyle FCE(

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}_{f}^{\prime},V_{s})

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{c}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}_{\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}V_{c}

Otherwise,
M
is a module expression. By definition of slicability,

M
does not syntactically contain any structure. In the general
case, we should proceed by induction over module expressions. We
will simply present the case of a mixed functor application
where the functor returns a mixed structure.

We consider
M=F(X_{1})\dots(X_{n})
.
We have

B.5.
Proof of the main theorem

Finally, we prove
Theorem

3
. This is a direct consequence of

Lemma

10
.

Proof of
Theorem

3
.

We have that
P\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \{\}\ }}v,\theta
. By definition of an

Eliom
ε
program execution, we can decompose this rule as following:

\frac{P\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \{\}\ }}_{m}(),\mu,\theta_{s}\quad\mu\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \{\}\operatorname{|}\varepsilon\rightarrow\gamma\ }}_{c}v,\theta_{c}}{P\operatorname{\xRightarrow{\ \{\}\ }}v,\theta_{s}\operatorname{@}\theta_{c}}

We trivially have the following invariants:

\displaystyle\{\}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}_{\{\}}\{\}

\displaystyle\{\}

\displaystyle\simeq^{s}\{\}

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}

\displaystyle\simeq^{c}\gamma

\displaystyle FCE(\{\},P)

\displaystyle FCE(\{\},\{\})

which allow us to apply
Lemma

10
and conclude.
∎
